LADIES' T;FTTER.

Mr DEAR Nitumt, In millinery, flat shapes may prevail, but any amount of height can be given to them, without destroying their fashionable contour, by means of bristle pompons or waving fronds of stiffened feather. 'Several layers of snuff- brown tulle com- pose the airy piece of headgear seen recently, which has a flat white rosette at one side, centred witt. a boss of pink coral beads and a white plame. People woud do a great service if they would forttiwith forswear all plumage that has been taken in violence and with pain from the birds it adorned. Whether the extr.mie flatness which characterises all the new millinery models will be 'popular remwins *to be seen. A spring hat is a pretty one in white Yeddah straw, with pale blue foulard, spotted with ring spots in mauve, draped round the brim, and a few blush-pink roees nestling in the folds at the back; a simple yet effective hat for &Lilly spring.

Sa e_,•i,-..ately pale are the newest spring materials *that they look ill-fitted to face the rigours of this month, yet, they are fascinatingly fresh after the distinct emerald greens and wood browns now .prevalent. A pretty cloth gown is made with an exceedingly graceful skirt, given a hip - yoke from which moderately full folds of a tunic bang, which in its turn is succeeded by two 'ounces, each edged with taffetas bands of a deeper -hyacinth purple Shade. The pouched bodice is crelihed by black and white silk embroideries on a purple taffetas background, and opens over a chemisette of pale orchid purple point d'esprit held at the waist with a multitude of tiny blaok buttons such as are seen on the sleeves that are se reennisoeut of the skirt. A cream white cap is suitable in its simplicity for the present month. with a bold band of black taffetas threaded through a purple enamel buckle, and a single pale purple rosette with short pleated ends tucked must one side.

lost pt the gowns worn this spring will be in neutral tones, all white, mostly enriched with gold and silver embroideries, mauve, and black and white. Brocades play a very important part for the. trains, which in most cases follow the manteati de cottr style, slang from both or one shoulder, and drooping down in the centre in graceful folds, an as fo reveal the back and waist. The edges of these trains are rolled and wadded, which give. them a substantial appearance, and prevent theta from becoming draggled and turning over. Beyond this rolled edge is a garniture of embroidery or appliqu6, or waved twists of gauze or chiffon. Ono very handsome trkin of thick ivory satin, wove. with a design of roses in silver, will have its edges trimmed with a twisted rope of silver gauze.

The woman who has not a purse of unlimited depth will be inquiring anxiously if the tailor coat and skirt—that ever-useful garment—is to be altered in style this spring. The fashion changes as much in these plainer clothes as in the finery of the more frivolous world. The most expensive tailor-mades are still severe in cut and style. The skirts sit closely round the hips, widen:ng out in flutes below the knees. The train is to be dispensed with for useful or morning wear, so as to leave the hand free from holding up the skirt in walking. A bolero, composed entirely of tucks overlapping each other and stitched with silk, will be worn with-the tailor-made. It will meet the bodice just below the armpits, and will add a very smart effect to the costume.

For a new morning dress, the material is of fire serge, ,made with a small basque. The collar is cut in the new approved shape out of applique and embroidered linen. The skirt is quite plain except for strappings. It is out in the latest style, fitting elosely to the hips, and is full round the feet. Trifies,of muslin and lace are, if possible, worn at this moment more than ever before, and there is nothing that makes a girl look more daintily dressed than the addition of one or other of these pretty collars or ruffles. The Parisian, who always excela in providing soMething peculiarly trim for neck wear, is continually bringing out new ideas a this sort. But even she does not indulge in a more fascinating variety of stocks for lokrases than those now adopted Tqa the Englishwoman who cares for her appearaill Whether made in silk or in velvet, in muslin or in chiffon, or in lace, they are always attractive. And what is more, a clever woman can make one for herself wii,h a very small outlay of time ot. mOnay. • . .

Your loving JENNLIg.

A KIND-HEARTED lady read in the papers that a little boy had been run over and seriously injured while endeavouring to rescue a cat which had flown into the roadway. The lady straightway, sent a plump cheque inclosed. in a note complimenting the bravery of the youthful hero, directed to him the hospital where he was being patched up. B. return of post she received an acknowledgment from the matron, who thoughtfully added that perhaps the lady was not aware that the cat nu:tied by the good little boy was a tip•cat !

CIT.APTER IV.

More than two months base elapsed since we first saw Minna and her cousin riding past the old par- sonage garden on that pleasant day in the early aut- umn. In vain Frau von Tiircke protested against the creel fate that condemned her to shiver through the bitter November days at Schloss Medemburg instead of hurrying away- to her luxurious villa on the sunny banks of the Arno; the poor woman had long since realised that her daughter's will was law, and when Minna calmly announced that their visit would be prolonged, she knew that from such a decision there pould be no appeal. If only the dearest wish of her peart—the union of the cousins—had been gratified by this sacrifice then, indeed, she would have fait some compensation, but there was nothing lover- like in Conrad's brotherly attentions- and even those Minna treated sometimes With cold indifference, sometimes with capricious disdairt. At the parson- age, when she chose to call there, she was received with mingled feelings characteristics of its inmates; the old pastor, absorbed in his books, heedless of petty social distinctions, regarded the stately young city beauty with much the 'same kindly paternal in- terest he would have shown towards Gertrud llillern and Frieda Schmidt; the good Fran Pastor, be- wildered and flurried at so unexpected an honour, trusted that " the gniidiges Friiulein " would pardim the poverty of their humble home, and inwardly hoped that the thread-bare carpet and shabby furni- ture might pass unobserved. As for Elsa, forgetful of self, she gazed in silence at the proud, beautiful features of their visitor and thought with a sudden sharp pain she could not analyse, still less under- stand, that it was no wonder if Baron Conrad loved is cousin, and were loved by her in, return. As time passed on Fraulein von Tfircke diet not rest content with a casual acquaintance but seemed bent upon establishing an intimacy between herself and the pastor's daughter, and the two girls, so strangely dissimilar, not only in social position but in every feature of mind and person, had become fre- quent companions. Not the least of Elsa's natural gifts was her lovely voice, a pure high soprano, sweet as the liquid notes of a nightingale, clear as a silver bell; it had been a lab Our of love with Friiu- loin Alpes to cultivate this voice, and under her careful guidance it had been trained and perfected, and made an instrument of pleasure to all who heard her sing. But never before had it been in such re- quisition as now ; Minna declared that no voice she had ever heard harmonised so well with her own full rich contralto as Elsa's bird-like notes; and in the long dreary November days when the monotonous rain fell in torrents and the gray rniAs rising up from the valley blotted out the distant landscape, the weather would be forgotten while Fraulein Agnes presided at the piano, and stately ohorales or lively operatic airs, the love-songs of Mozart and the sweet melodies of Iterdslssolin wiled the hours sway. _ _ _

Through all this close intereourse, havever. Elsa Lorentz experienced at times a elisions feeling of re- straint between herself and Frei:dein Minim; there were moments when, looking up suddenly, she had found the black eyes of the latter Axed urn her with a gaze as of slow smouldering fire in tbsir storm(deeths, a gaze that filled her with e la!ent sense of dread, and recalled with a shudder that day whop' she stood in the parsonage garden and watchell the eiders pass. It was especially when Baron,Coe - rad joined the circle that Elsa was most conscious of this sensation of terror and unrest; then, with the -w ay ward capriciousnessthat characterised her actions, Minna would fling down her music, declare that she was tired, and insist upon her cousin singing in her stead, and would sit apart, and watch in brooding silence for some sign that should convince her that the love she craved for with all the strength of her passion- ate nature had been given to another than herself. But these doubte that haunted her were, baffled by Elsa's child-like innocence, and by the restraint which that very innocence and maidenly dignity im- posed upon Baron von Medem himself. Elsa be 7 lieved that Minna was the Baron's chosen bride, and so the three, whose lives were for the time so closely interwoven, were yet in thought as widely separ- ated as though they had been strangers to each other, and the fateful game of cross-purposes in which unwittingly each had 9 part, was pldyed on blindly until it reached a hitter end. . As the old year drew towards it; close, for the first time or many years the Schloss was filled with .geests, the old walls echoed with gaiety end mirth, and even Frau von 'Pumice's querulous complaints were lessened as she saw •eroutid some of the familiar laces she had never ceased to miss, and heard fresh items of news from the outside -world, to live apart from which had seemed to her perpetual exile. Among the other visitors CAllle that Count Hochstadt whose Arne we have already heaid as a suitor for Minna von Tdreke's hand: he was a man of noble lamily and of larkse fortune, one whose lofty position and unblemishee. character plie.:ed him high in the favour of his Sovereign ; at first sight there would have appeared to be little in common between the grave, middle-aged soldier and the beautiful, faseinatine- 'woman in die zenith of her youth, hut who can explain or understand the spell Love weaves around his victims.? Count Hochstadt was not the first, as he will not be he last, to be deceived by a woman's wiles, and so long as the world endures, no man will ever be deterred by nother's folly front straying into the same fool's paradise himself. Minna von Tiireke was ambitious, but it waseui mere ambition or mercenary motive that swayed her actions at this juncture ; never 'before hi her whole iife had she known what it was to have her will ~hwarted,stlierefore never before had she so' ardent- ly longed for what was denied to her as now. 'That she should have betomwed her love where it WIS un- sought and undesired, was in itself a bitter evenilia- firm, but when, as time passed on, Conrad remained still indifferent, nay rather, as she suspected, loved elsewhere, there was a sudden revulsion, and pride came to her aid , he should learn how other men prized as a royal Oft the favour that was valueless to him, and by their ardour should be taught to covet what he had hitherto despised. It was thus then that matters stood on that January morning when Count Hochstadt put his fortune once more to the test, and something in the expree- eion of his face warned Minna that idle could no longer trifle with this man who pleaded for his an- swer in such impassioned tones, and that whether for happiness or misery before the day was over her fate must be decided.

The rendez roi-t-s-ti-gi:eed upon was a small lake lying high up in tie very heart of the 'forest, some two hours' drive from Schloss Alletlemburg • it was a spot so weird and isolated in its surroundings, so far apart from the. haunts er. civilisation, that it was not styprising the Maintain-See WaS believed by the ig- norant and superstitious pea.:antry to be haunted, and.that many a wild and fanciful legend had been woven of the water,soirita Who mqde .their home within its dep,tbs. The name was derived from an old local tradition that at one end of the lake, deep &own below its 'lnflate, a sulphor spring issued from the hillside enly .to lose itaelf again by a subterranean autlet. At certain seaseas a curious phenomenon is observable in the Brannen-See: on all sides but one the forest. cover:al hills slope steeply down to the scostee's edge, but if the traveller should chance to be: standing on that one • narrow margin of shingly beach open a still summer's day, when not a breath of air stirs the motionless leaves, and when the calm surface of the lake lies smooth as a mirror :at glass reflectlng every white cloudlet floating lazily in the blue sky overhead, and every green tree bend- ing dewu to its twin duplicate below, he will be startled all at once'hy r sudden ripple. rising at the far end and spreading over the surface of the water, coming nearer and nearer and gaining in strength until it breaks at length in it tiny wavelet upon the pebbles at his feet; this is repeated at irregular inter- vals, the ripples not alw.iy.esettingin the same direc- HOP, hut thsvayS 'ionbrieue,ng Irom the same point at the further end' of the Take. '

The traveller's thoughts may lie will turn involun- tarily to that old story of a sacred pool where "art angel w-nt down at a certain season aud troubled the Mater' giving it. a healing power from his touch, bui: his peasant guide will cross hhnself with a shudder, and whisper, below his breath, that there' is no tatgel here, but that the " nixies " are angry with the venturesome mortal who has dared to in- vade their solitude, and are hurrying up from the Devil's Spring on the crest of the little waves, to try and frighten him away. 'A Tess romantic explanation may, or may not, be found in the eiistenee of the aforesaid spring which rises with intermittent power from its subterranean source.

When only one sledge remained of all that had crowd. ad the quadrangle, and the sound of jinglimy; bells grew fainter and fainter in the distance, Conrad von Medem returned to his former position under the portico at the head of the broad flight of steps ; scarcely three minutes elapsed before hurried foot- steps were heard traversing the inner hall ; von Modem turned at the sound, and throwing away his cigar advanced to greet the new-comer. THE UNIVERSITY BOAT RACE: THE RIVAL STROKES.

As usual, the Boat 'nee, as it is always called, as though it were the only one of the whole year, is exciting. plenty of interest this year, and the

training .o-f ithe 'crews has been wawtehed with a

great deal of interest. Now that we are on the eve of the race the usual variations in the favour of first one and then the other are lost sight of in the general anticipation, and the final judgment is that the race will be contested by a very fair pair of crews of almost - equal merit. FO that it should prove a close ,and interesting fight. EN er since the Trial Eights at Oxford and Cambridge last term it has been obvious that the iat•.;ei University had more stuil to draw upon, azai better lot of men from which to form a ere w. .but Oxford, although perilously short of first- class oars, has been able to find just enongli to make a good crew, - though- there is hardly -another one .p there at present. It has ben close work in it way.. but it has largely simplißcd the -question of selection, and their boat was con-. eequently able to settle down and get into trim long before the Light Blues. The latter, however, have shaped better .Tiora the very start, and have been favourites all along, but clurin the last few days their rivals have come on \vial such a spurt that it is now expected that they will make a good fight for it, if not actually ,successful. Mr: F. O. J Huntley (T_TniVersity) last year's bow, is stroking the Dark Blues, and, though he has perhaps made more progress recently than anyone else-in the eight,-he is hardly an ideal stroke. In fact, the death of Mr. Culme-Seymour, last lyeer's stroke, who 'won the-race so wonderfully by pure pluck aid generalship, has been an absolutely irreparable loss, as Oxford had counted upon hit% for at least another two years, and have not been able-to replace him, as at present they have no real 'stroke—a thing that is born, not made up just mow. However, they are lucky' in possessing Mr. Maclagan, who is steering them for the fourth successive year and so ably aided Qulm&Seymottr to victory last year. It is a great point in the battle to have a cox who knows the way of the ThanaeS and the tricks of the Putney-to- Mortlake course, as Mr. Maclagan does, while the Cambridge steersman, Mr. Wasborough,' though well thought of, is new to the inter-Varsity contest. Strangely enough, the Cambridge stroke, Mr. R. H. Nelson, also rowed bow for his TJoniversity, but he, too, has fitted well into his new\ position and rows with much dash and vigour. The rest of the Cambridge crew are as follows; (7) Mr, Edwards Moss, a very fair oar but inclined to be short - (he is a member of the celebrated Oxford 'vowing family and it is a great surprise to all row- ing men to find him at tka rival University) ; (6) Mr. H. B. Grylle, an old blue and one of the best men in the boat; (5) Mr. F. J. Escombe, a promising novice from Trinity Hall; (4) Mr. C. H. Taylor' a - very powerful oar, if not a very elegant one; (3) Mr. .I'. Thomas, who has improved since being shifted from the seven thwart ; (2) Mr. Drysdale, of Jesus. another good novice in first-class rowing; and -Mr. W. H. Chapman at bow, who rowed in the same position in the 1899 crew, since when he has, been serving in South Africa. He is a very hard worker and well thought of. The Oxford crew behind Mralluntley are: (7) Mr. Adams, a credit- able car; !I) Mr. A. de L. Long, an improved and excellent old blue; (5) Mr. J. Milburn, who, with Mr. D. Milburn (2), hail from America, and have learned all their rowing at -Oxford, both most promising oars; (4) Mr. H. J. Hale. the 0.H.8.C. president, who has proved a little disappointing; (a) Mr. J. Younger, another old blue and very neat oar, and Mr. Drinkwater at bow, who rows far more strongly than he appears to. Thus it will be seen that each 'Varsity have four old colours and some promising new men, out of which two very fair crews have been got- together. Both have been admirably coached, and, if there is anything to choose between them, it is in favour of the Light Blues, who have done more uniformly well. The ways of the War Office are devious, and to the layman it is difficult to comprehend , their purpose in ordering Sir James Willcocks out to the Front with no little mystery, and shipping him home again immediately upon landing, to go on to Ladle to take up a command there. It is absurd for the officials to play battledore and shuttlecock in this ' Way with our generals and the public purse. The Whole proceeding has been so eminently Childish as to arouse a good deal of unfavourable comment. IHE EXAMINER. FRIBA N, MARCH

ANNUAL MEETING OF WARRING- TON INFIRMARY GOVERNORS.

Ifrom Mr Tunstall :—" Heathfield,Latcbford,fith March, 1902. Dear Sir,—lt is with the greatest regret that I find it necessary to ask the Governors of the Warring- ! ton lutirmary and , Dispensary to acoept at their next mee.ing my resignation of the hou secretaryship of the institution. For some time past I have been in in- different health, and I am now, indeed, recovering but slowly from a severe illness, which will unfit me for business for a censiderable time to come. My chief reason, however, for asking to be relieved of my duties is the magnitude of the work involved in their satis- factory performance, and the Irripracticability or my coping longer with it. The uniform courtesy and kind- ness which I have experienced in my intercourse with the Board of Management and Its individual members, and for which I sincerely thank them,will always cause me the most live!y satisfaction, and arouse in me the pleasantest of memories. I need hardly say that I shall be glad, if desired, to continue my services for a short time pending the appointment oi my successor.— I am, dear sir, faithfully yours, J hTIINSTALL. J C Parr, Esq., J.P., C.C., President Warrington Infirmary and Dispensary." . The MAYOR said he need not comment upon the regret with which the Board had received this communication not only because they were losing a valued and efficient officer hut because of the mann given by Mr Tunstall, that his health was in so indifferent a state that tt would be a long time before he undertook such duties again. They all anew the long service be bad devoted to the Infirmary and the manner in which:be bad given up his time and sacrificed himself, if he might so express it, for the good of the institution. A good deal of its efficiency and the good work it bad done was due to his devotion and the manner in whith he bad looked after its affairr. He was not lila position to say more it pre- sent. The Board of Manatement would take the matter in hand, and he need now only express his great grati- tude to Mr Tunstall for his kindly suggestion that be would carry them over the present difficulty by continu- ing his service as befere. (Applause.) The Rey Jean YONGE expressed. the hope that this was not an absolute resignation. The mere fact that Mr Tunstall was willing to serve for a short time gave them the hope that if his health were restored, which they all trusted, he would be willing to take on the office again. They would immensely regret to lose hiss, and he hoped the Board of Management would take tbe liberty of pressing upon Mr Touted! their great desire that he should continue in the position. The MAYOR said it might be left to the Board of Management to carry out that suggestion. It was decided to leave the matter in the bands of the Board of Management to deal with as they thought fit. A Satisfactory Year.

The Council Chamber at the Town Hall, Warrington, was cpmpletely filled on Tuesday afternoon on the occa- sion of the annual meeting of Governors. A Warrington Will Suit.

In the Chancery Division on Saturday before Mr Justice Swinfen Eady, the case of Birch v. Tayleur, in re Mr 11 T Tayleur, deceased, of Warrington,was heard. Mr Rutherford appeared for tha plaintiffs, who he stated were the trustees of the will of Mr T Tayleur and the point which was raised in the summons was as to whether a sum of ..E2,000 paid by the testator for shares in the Vulcan Foundry Ca, Limited, and which were transferred to his son Henry layleur, should be deducted as an advanee under the will. The testator gave the residue of his estate in equal shares to his two sons, and the rest of the protoerty to the four daughters. When the estate became divisible the son in respect of whom the :question now arose was dead, and the residual legatees were parties to this action.— Mr Cochrane,who appeared for Mr R Tsyleur and Florence Woods, argued that this sum was only an advance, sad ought to be brought into hotch-pot. The evidence was that Edward Tayleur bought some shares from his brother, the testator, and by direction of the testator he transferred them into the name of his son, who received the dividends directly. It was quite clear in the account that the shares were originally bought for the testator himself ,and that when in account with his brother he was entitled to the transfer of them he directed that they should be transferred to the son, and this was done.—Mr James, who appeared on behalf of the beneficiaries under the will, and the executor of the deceased brother, contended that there was not su cient evidence to convince. the Court that these shires transferred to the son were the sham referred to. He understood that the son bad an unfortunate railway accident by which both his legs were cut off, and that the shares in question were transferred to him by the father because in consequence of this accident be was unable to earn his own living.—After further argument Mr Justice Hedy held that the shares must be treated as an ad- vance and brought into the account.

AN/MIA CURED IN LANCASHIRE.

Too ill to go to Work—Young Woman who feels like a

Different Person Now.

Females are frequent victims of the ailment aris— ing from sudden changes from the hot atmosphere of the factory to the cold air of the streets, and a few days ago a Bolton reporter heard of one remark- able case, that of Miss Martha Turner, who lives with her parents at No. 29, Pilkington-st., off Derby- street, Bolton. On visiting the above address the reporter was fortunate enough to find Miss Turner at home, and he was surprised to find her looking re- markably well ; in fact, wearing the perfect broom of health upon her cheeks.

"Well,' said the reporter, "I have heard accounts of a very remarkable experience which you have had, and thought I would investigate the matter for myself, and if possible get the story first hand."

With a cheerful welcome Miss Turner then invited the visitor inside, and with the utmost readi- ness consented to give the facts.

"Sometime ago," she began, " I was in very poor health, suffering from shbrtness of breath, and a great feeling of weakness and debility. I was going from bad to worse when, fortunately, I heard of Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People, which quite cured me. I struggled to my work as long as I possibly could, but at last had to give in. I tried all kind of medicines, but nothing seemed to do me any good. I was beginning to think that nothing would set me right again, as the weakness and listlessness seemed to be taking. a firmer hold on me as time went on. I became very pale—in fact,appeared quite bloodless, and f quickly tired from even slight exertion. I lost my appetite to a great extent, and the food I took did not seem to pull me up at all.

Fortunately I was advised in time to take Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People, and was told of the great benefit which other mill hands who had suffered like I was, had derived from them. I had read about the pills in the newspapers, but had never paid much attention to them. However, as I got no better, I decided to give Dr. Williams' Pink Pills a trial, and I was delighted to find that from the very first they began to do me good. I persevered with them for some time, and took several boxes, by which time I was quite well and strong again and able to go back and continue at my work. Since then I have been a different person. 'if I feel at all bad now, or feel any signs of shortness of breath, I get a box of Dr. Williams' Pink Pills, and they set me right directly. The paleness in my cheeks and lips gave place to a better colour, the aching in my limbs disappeared, and my appetite improved. My food, too, seemed to give me strength, and I soon felt as well and strong as I have ever felt nmy life.

In further conversation Miss Turner, a remarkably intelligent young woman, said she had re- commended Dr. Williams' Pink Pills to all her friends.

"Then you don't mind me making use of your statement for the benefit of others," said the reporter. "Certainly not," replied Miss Turner. "1 have already advertised the matter amongst my friends as much as possible, and shall continue to do so."

During the greater portion of the conversation above recorded, Miss Turner's mnther was present. She corroborated all that her daughter had said, and added that she was so satisfied at her daughter's case that she had recommended Dr Williams' rink rills to many people, and was now taking them herself for indigestion and stomach complaint.

LECTURE ON JAPAN.

`'Windsor Magazine " ; which also contains articles on i "The Poland of To-ease" " Training a Sheep-dog," By the Captain of the English Team. 1 "Steeplejacks," " A Remarkeble Collection of Chess- " I have always strenuously held that the front row men," " Animals at church," " A Cure for Drunken- of the serammage should lock tight, but this advice ness " ; together with the opening chap'ers of a power. allows of reserveoioe. In saying that be front row i tut new serial," "In a Fog," be Fichard Harding Davis. should bind tight, I must insist that the binding, and an exciting instalment of Cuteliffe Hyne's "Tho ne- although tight, must be able to undo in a second. For- son's Progress." wards must be able to break up immediasely, both for purposes of attack and defence ; and to be able to break up at a given moment without delay the ball must be watched. Forwards have got to remember that dinctly the ball is out of the scrummage they must be out as well. The shoving must continue till the last moment, but when the ball has gone, break up and help your behiuds. Of course, the wbeel and the quick rushes of towards depend almost entiri ly on the quickness with which the scrummage is broken up. Also, forwards must bear in mind that they have .got to help in the defence. When your opponents have got the hall and begin their passing moments, all the tor wards must be at hand to help the behinds. How often I have seem a half or Urine-quartets run iingle-trandel through a whole side of forwards, who simply stand stolidly still and stare at him, without ever thinking of laying a hind on him, cr perhaps one, a little more awake than the rest, will make a grab at his neck, or hair, or ear. But a forward must tackle, tackle always, and tackle hard. High tackling is, as a rule, quite useless. The place to tackle is eibher just above the knees or round the waist. The latter is perhaps the preferable, as the ball may be held at the same time. Bat the great thing in all tack- ling, for a forward or a behind, is to tackle hard and low, and to always go for the man with the ball. Never mind the otter people who may get the tall, go for the man who has got Pe There are tar too many forwards just at the present moment who are very weak in tackling, which is ale of the most important points in the Rugby game. Gentle tackling is useless (I don't wish anybody to misunderstand ma and think I am advocating roughness) ; you must throw yourself at your man and down him. One often sees quite a good tackler miss a mem because he does not throw himself at him. It is fatal to taink you can never tackle a cer- tain individual, for then you never try. Make up your mind to go for him and bring him down at all °este. To me there is always great pleasure in bringing off a good tackle. There is a feeling of satisfaction about it that ' does me a lot of good and makes me try harder than ever tor the rest of the game. Weak tackling amongst foewards is one of the most promiennt features of the Rugby game in the South. One frequently sees a for- ward or several forwards make a fine dribble and at the end kick the ball perh3ps a trifle hard ; then, instead of leaping on the full-back or whoever the player may be Who has obtained possession of the ball, they appear paralyeed,and quite possibly all the ground is lost again. A great number of forwards imagine that so long as they get the ball out to the backs,it makes no difference how, when, or where it comes out of the scrummage, and that once they have got rid of ir, the responsibility is off their hands for a while ; but this is very far from being the case, tor a great deal niviire depends upon the prows heeling out of the ball than most people imagine. How often one sees the ball Come out of the side of a scrummage instead of in the very middle of the back row ! How often when it does come out at the beck it is shot out like a rocket and with a force that carries It yards past the halves, or else it takes, at the other extreme, about a minute to get through the various rows of legs that bar its way,giving the otMosing halves heaps of time to creep round and follow it up! The ball rnust be heeled out properly, cleanly, and quickly, or else the forwards don't give their halves and three- quarters a chance. When the forwards have obtained , the ball and have got orders to heel it out, it is most : important that they should still continue shoving. It is quite impossible and useless to heel the ball if the for- , wards are getting shoved. Keep shoving, and send the : ball back quicely, avoiding letting it touch the legs if possible, and not giving it violent kicks back. Of course, it is sometimes absolutely necessary to help it on its way back, but it should be done carefully with a push, sa as to avoid driving it too hard for the halves to get hold of. Another excellent method is to shove straight when you have got the ball and walk over it. By this means you give your halves more time,while also placing the other halves on toe defensive behind their own men The great thing is, however, net to let the ball come out at the side, as this does not give your own halves, who are following behind you a chance, while it fele right in the path of your opponents. If a forward at the aids of the scrimmage sees this ball going out at the silo, he must hook it back into the scrimmage at once, and then the forwards must try again and heel it out properly. Never lct it come out at the side. It is far better to wait a few seconds and have it done properly, than to think that the great thing is speed and that it does not matter when the ball comes out. Personally, I censideref your pack is strong enough,that the best way of heeling is the one I have just mentioned—i.e.oi walk- ing over the ball."— From an article by J Daniell on " Rugby Football," in the March number of the

Proceeding, he said they had not done very much in the past for toreign missions although they had always manaeed to send a few pounds every year to tne S. P.O. Missionary work did not occupy the piece in Lieir midst it was entitled to occupy. It was riot easy to find money for everything, but at tue setae time they should make the best of their opportunities arid give what they could with the object of heining on mission work. They should not forget that Eaglani itself was Cbristianized by missionaries from abroad. So it Wa9 with Ireland. and he was reminded that that day was the festival of that great Irish missionary St Patrick, who came Co Ire- land from Scotland. Ever sinc •he (the Vicar) came to Warriaetm the parish had bees receiving assistance from one of the great flame Morsion Societies. Re was referring to the Auditionel Curates' Society, which for 23 years to his knowledge, had been giving grants for the maintenance of the clergy in the parish. They were receiving assistance at the present time. They would all agree with him that that was a very strong reasen for their being interested in such a society. He (the speaker) thought the time a convenient one to form a missionary asscciation, which would coal blue the causes of both home and foreign RI SaiOUS. The work would be the same in both cases, and it would be far better to concentrate their efforts.--L-specially when t leir resources were so limited. filer might aiso in:roduce the box system. Toe misiionary-boxes could be placed in the homes of parishioners and the members of the tainily r could Contribute something when they so desired or wished to exercise a litt:e self-Jenial. (Applause.) Ths lsC:urer,at the 0 ict,reterred to the physical char- acteristics of Japan. He pointed out that it was very mountainous, many of the mountains exceeding 10,000 feet in heigh'. Mouy of the mountains were regarded as sacred by the Japanese who erected temples on the mountains and at stated periods hundreds of pilgrims proceeded to worship there. In a!luding to the Buddhist priests he said many of them subsisted by begging. They were despised as a class and had a bad ref utation for their private hype. The Buddhist priests made a pro- fession of the:r calling whilst the iSbinto prie its engaged in other occupations and weie aressed as ordinary men except wheu they were performing their religious functions. As a ciass the Shinto priests were more respected. Some excellent views were shown of the temples, th., houses and the costumes of the Japanese. Proceeding te refer to what was known as •he old English Church in Tokyo be said it was built in 1378, but owing to an earthquake it was considered unsafe and in UM it was pulled down. It was fifteen years. he said, since the riret missionaries went out. :St Andrew 'e Mission consisted of English clergy who looked afier mission churches, taught in the Christian schools, and sperforMed other kinds of wok. The population of Tokyo was 14 millions and a visitor was atee to walk eight miles in a straight line without going outside the city. In addi- tion to the work carried on in connecion with StAndrew's mission, some excellent work among women and girls was being done by St Hilda's missioa,conducted by tatty missionaries. The lecturer pointed out that mud] of the missionary work was of an educational character, especially amongst the young. Many were being train sci for mission work and substantial progress was being made. At the close, the CHAIRMAN proposed a vote of thanks to Mr Webb for his lecture and to Mr Henry Taylor for the nee and manipulation at the lantern. He said Mr Taylor was always willing to come to their assistance on occasions Irks the present, and almost every part of Warrington was indebted to him for his kindness in that and many ether ways. (Applause).

FIFTY YEARS DEAF. ___

" The Journal for the Deaf" has often reported the brilliant suc3ess of the Drouet methods in the treatment of Deafness and ear diseases cf long standing, but io the February "Supplement " there is an ac3o.int of the case of Mrs Clayton, whose cure is reported after a life- time of defective hisarinz,, a cure that can be rightly termed a triumph for this specialised system of aural treatment. The Deafness in Mrs Clayton's case was of extreme character, and accompanied by continuous noises in the head. Her last communication to the Drauet Inutitute contains the proof of • complete cure : _

95, Eianover-treet. Sheffield, Jan. 23rd, MI IDear Sir,—l am sorry to say that the general impres- lion is that there is no cure for Deafri, es, but aince I had your treatment I have changed my, opinion. Report I nay case by all means, and I hope it may induce some- one to seek relief for a very distressing affliction.— Sincerely yours, S. H. CLAYTON. PA.—Mention in your report that I have been bfilict- id from my infancy. A very complete report f this case, together with a description of a number of other cured cases, of differ- ent varieties of Deafness and ear, nose and throat diseases, can be found in the February " Supplement to the Journal for the Deaf." A free co,ey of this publica- tion can be obtained by addressing tne Secretary of the I Drouet Institute, Regent's Park-road, London. N.W. I The Journal is accompanied by a Patient's Repot t Form, which enables anyone suff,ring from any diaease of the nose, throat, or ears, to submit their case for the advice of the Conkteting Paysician. No charge i made for this rate ation on the part of the Poyincian, and all advice by correspondence is absolutAy free.

HALTON'S BRIDGE.

Mr Snoddy asked of any reply had been received to the Council' letter of the 3rd inst. and upon being told that none had been received, he proposed that the Council again press for a reply, trusting that the City Council would see their way to proceed with the work promptly. Ire so doing the County Council would dispense with one of the moat dangerous bend on a highway in that part of the county. This was seconded by Mr Clarke, and agreed to. ____•,______ Child's Sad Death.

Mr S Brighouse conducted an inquest at the W tenon on Tuesday night,relative toatrhreindgetiM Police fliPatrick Gray, aged four years, ot 27 Allcard-atreet, who died at the Warrington infirm- ary on the 13th inst.—Detective Barlow read out the statement of deceased's mor, her, who was not present. It was to the effect that at two o'clock on the let inst the deceased was playing in the back yard, and got on a chair. He overbalanced himself and fell to the ground,slightly cutting his eyebrow. He did not appear any the worse for it until the 10th mat, when the eye became very swollen. Dra Nuttall and Liston were called in, and the latter ordered deceased'a removal to the infirmary.—Nurae Garner said the deceased was admitted to the In- firmary on the 12th inst suffering from blood- poisoning about the head aud face, and died the following day.--A verdict of " Accidental death " was returned.

THE EXAMINER.

MARCH 21s' 1902.

Since the days when Mr The Admirelte and W. H. SMITH was satir- Wei-Hai Wei. ised in "H.M.S. fore" as "the rnler of the QUEEN'S Navee," there has keen a:general belief that the First Lord of the Admiralty, whoever he may be, is a lay figure con- trolled by the experts of the Naval Board. Lord SELBORNE in the interesting debate as to Wei- Hai-Wei in the House of Lord on Tuesday evenin. something to modify that opinion. Without unduly asserting himself he conveyed that ho had been responsible for a change of policy as te Wei-Hai-Wei which will probably affect other places, being careful, however, to strengthen hint' self with expert advice before he took the stet', and also to secure the support of the Cabinet. In a word Lord SELBORNE appears as the chief who weighs the arguments of conflicting opinions, and decides the issue for himself.: Lord SELBORNE'S speech was an epitome Of Captain MAHAN'S theories; and although lie; did not mention that eminent naval authoritYp, it is easy to read between the lines and to see • that he had drawn on the captain for his imprer sions and had placed them before his naval ad' 1 visers, with the result already mentioned. The ; substance of his case for the change was the prin- ciple that ships are better than bricks, because; the ships are movable, and can render effective service in various places, whereas fortifications avail in only one place. This consideration is, a course, of limited application ; but LORD SO' BORNE stated definitely that the abandonment of the intention to make Wei-Hai-Wei a primal naval base was determined prior to the Japanese Treaty. The British Government had no thought of the assistance Japan could give for the protee." tion of British interests. It is quite probable that in a time of naval conflict Wei-Hai-Wei would be left practically undefended if the stress of the engagement were in other waters, and Russia might slip in and occupy the station. Bet on the other hand if the place were unfortified itS weakness would tell in our favour for recapture, because until elaborate work was undertaken it would not be tenable against the attacks o vessels, and we should be able to recapture it when our ships were free to do so. LORD Set- BORNE is, in fact, a convert to the theory that,, mobility of naval resources is the most important thing. All this may be true. It is a matter to be argued out by the experts of rival schools of naval thought. But it is unfortunate that the Government should not have been able to come te a decision before committing itself so definitely. All sorts of conjectures have been caused by the 1 alteration, the most unfortunate of which work) that our Admiralty did not know its own mind a that the first proposals as to Wei-Hai-Wei were s hollow piece of stage show. Before Lord Goscueel took the step he did he must have consulted the Cabinet, and when there was so much divergence of opinion among experts as thine must have been even then Ministers should not have shouted se loud. Everyone remembered Mr. Balsoun's high • note of satisfaction to which Lord Roseseer re- ferred in a recent debate on this subject. Thie was supposed to be a brilliant check to Russian , aggression. Now we realise that the chief use of Wei-Hai-Wei is to be as a sanatoripm and a train' ing place. It is well to confess an error of judg- went as early as possible rather than to pursue e blunder, and that is the best that can be said fec Lord SELBORNE. But this country profits most by a quiet resolute line, and the alternative prac' tice of graceful concessions and flights of Jing,e oratory,followed by admissions of their bollownese is not calculated to inspire respect for us among other nations.

send out another Sir HENRY STANLEY to discover Darkest Africa. The Lard CHANCELLOR admitted the arrival of a half-hundredweight of returns as to the vorkings of martial law in Cape Colony,but discouraged any attempt to put a date to their sorting. The British public, both as relatives of soldiers who are fighting and in their capacity as taxpayers, desire information as to the supply of horses' food' and other things. Mr BRODRICK puts his foot down angrily and says the demand is unfair. The answer seems quite irrelevant, and would be absurd if it were not based on the assumption that the object of the critics was to gibbet officers who are engaged in administration. In reality it is nothing of the kind. Those who moved for the committee of investigation have done so on grounds of efficiency and economy, and not from any vindictive motives or from love of scandal. Whenever Liberals urge that steps should be taken to avoid waste and stay blunder- ing they blre told they are unpatriotic. Sir HENRY CAMPBELL-BANNERMAN read a military report which showed that up to a very recent date the remounts were still unsatisfactory to the military experts at the Cape. "Wait until the war is over," replies Mr BRODRICK. This is a mere pre- text for evasion. Toe disclosures of the profits of contractors show what a splendid thing they have found the war to be. Contracts for war purposes are speculations, but when a company can pay a dividend of 30s on El shares and can put one million sterling to the reserve it seems to have been given more than an ample margin. To say that after such a revelation the Opposition critics are led by mere motives of exposure in their desire for a supervising committee is begging the point. There have been quite enough exposures. What the House of Commons is concerned about is that in face of them the administration shall be shown be on a business-like footing. It is an insult to the House of Commons to suggest that any committee would condemn an officer in his absence. Its concern as to the past would be mainly to secure improvement in the future. There are too many evidences that the war is likely to go on for months; and just as com- mercial men would take stock of a serious situa- -tion, so Parliament should look carefully to its materials for supplying the military machine.

--- KING EDWARD'S first The King's First Court, which was held in Court so much splendour at Buckingham Palace, came as an augury of the Coronation festivities, and as a happy omen of the lustre which the new SOVEREIGN hopes to shed over his reign. Toe interest in the event was largely increased by the knowledge that the KING had taken the trouble to concern himself as to the details beforehand, and had prided himself in seeing that it should in every way be worthy of the traditions of the Court of St. James. On occasions, when the diplomatic corps is invited to meet subj sets of the KING who are most distin- guished in rank and other eminence, the function has an importance not only for the SOVEREIGN but for all the people over whom he rules. When his MAJESTY succeeded to the Throne he emphasised his intention of limiting his authority strictly to that of a constitutional monarch, and everyone has admired how, while keeping himself above all the party and other con- troversies of the hour, he has exercised his august influence for his country., Those only who are in the Court's secrets know how keen and varied are his activities, and how he aids his Ministers in the difficult tasks of international diplomacy. The increasing regard for him shows, however, that this is understood though it cannot be stated. The finest tribute which could be paid to any SOVEREIGN was the remark which Lord SALISBURY made when the country was mourning the death of the late QUEEN. "A. Minister," he said, "felt a great responsibility when he ventured to advise her MAJESTY against her first opinion on any matter that was brought before her." King EDWARD has not sought a life of leisure. His ambition has been to be worthy of his exalted position by inti- mate acquaintance with the matters affecting its larger responsibilities, and also with the smaller points—the little details which are so marked by Continental critics. In the Court held at Bucking- ham Palace he was in reality doing service for the country before the eyes of all the world as repre- sented by the Ambassadors. At other times he finds pleasure in giving pleasure to one or another portion of the community or class of his subjects as on the occasion of the visit to Plymouth, when he gratified alike the naval and civil elements by his graciousness. Happily, too, he has lent his influence in a special degree to movements for the alleviation of sickness and suffering. His people should findsatisfaction in his delight when releas- ing himself for a while from kingly duties in taking the part of a simple English gentleman, enjoying the pursuits of a country gentleman, discussing agricultural topics with the farmers round Sand- ringham.

Employment throughout the country remains wonderfully steady, despite the dismal prophecies of the pessimists. I year ago a decline seemed to have set in, which some people attributed to a diversion of capital to South Africa. Whatever the cause, the movement was checked during the summer months, since which time only very shght variations have had to be repur ted.During February the returns from the Labour Department of the Board of Trade shew that some improvement took place ; and, considering the time of the year, and the fact that the month included a period of frost {luring which outdoor occupations were seriously interfered with, this must be pronounced more than satisfactory? The state of employment is, however, still slightly worse than a year ago, for while the coal, iron and steel, and cotton industries have improved, there has been a certain decline in engineering, shipbuilding, building and certun other groups of trades. However, employ- ment is above the mean percentage for February for the last; ten years. Turning to the. changes of wages, we find that the net result is a decrease affecting rather more than 100,000 w. orkpeople but the amount is very small, averaging only 3d weekly per head. This, again, compares very favourably with the decline :of 2s per head affect- ing 200,000 workpeopla in February of last year. I.lnaployment may not be all that it might be wished, but there is, nevertheless, an even quality about the returns which is far preferable to a succession of alternate " boonas " and "slumps."

There is an interesting rumour to the effect that the Admiralty intead to take up Marconi's challenge, and demonstrate, even to the satisfac- tion of the reluctant inventor, that wireless rnes- ages on the most improved method can still be interfered with, intercepted, or rendered unin- telligible. The challenge was a very clear one. liarconi said definitely thakhe would undertake to send messages from his experimental station Which could not be intercepted by any ship other than that for which they were intended, To demonstrate his good faith he made various offers to place at the disposal of the sceptics a ship or station for a week for their experiments, but, of course, these would not,be necessary if the rumour ref erred to proves true. On the Vernon wireless

===== 0002601_19020321_art0059.txt | included=true | included =====
telegraphy has been studied for some time, and there are experts who could be trusted to make an investigation into the matter, while the fitting up and despatch of a vessel to test the matter would entail very little expense. So far, it need hardly be said, experiment in the Navy has run altogether counter to Mr Marconi's claims. Quito recently, the Revenge, now being refitted for Sir Gerard Noel's flagship at the Coronation review, got in touch while off Crook- haven with several mail steamers through her wireless telegraphy apparatus, and received also several messages which were being pßssed from the ships to the shore, or vice versa. But, even if Marconi's syntonic system can be established, would its adoption be an unmixed good? It would be sad if there should prove to be two ships on the high seas, within fifty miles, say, of each other, both fitted with Marconi's apparatus, and both with trained operators on board, and if the crew of one should perish for lack of assistance because communication between the two vesssls had been deliberately prevented by the tuning of the instruments to different keys.

The vaccination antidote has been much severer than the small-pox epidemic. One reported case was sufficient to send hundreds of Warrington people to the doctor with bared arms and trembling hearts. These were the cautious and the wise. The unwise and the less cautious laughed at the scare—which was but a scare after all—and have since been able to smile at their tender-armed brethren and sisters. The " mind my arm " cry associated with the last outbreak in Warrington has to a great extent been superseded by the rod ribbon—" the red badge of courage." For our own part we would rather have the old cry than the new ribbon,which is unnecessarily ostenta- tious. People can generally keep the injured limb out of harm's way easily ,enough, and there is no need to advertise the fact to all and sundry that one's left arm is to be avoided like the plague. There was almost a fashion ia ribbons and bows at first, but well-deserved ridicule is driving them away ' rapidly. There are still plenty of people whose natural repugnance to vaccination causes thorn to question its efficacy. Facts are stubborn things, however. Dr Hope, the Liverpool Medical Officer of Health, reported last week that at a certain lodging-house where small-pox appeared, seventy- one lodgers and fourteen of the staff were re-vaccin- ated,but thirteen of the lodgers ref used. Not a single one of the seventy-one lodgers or of the fourteen members of the staff who were re-vaccinated took small-pox, but of the thirteen lodgers who refused re-vaccination, nine had developei the disease. It was also pointed out that not one of the numerous staff of nurses and servants engaged at the small-pox hospital had contracted the disease.

MATTERS OF MOMENT.

A Provincial Letter.

Provincial Organizations for duly and adequately celebrating the forthcoming Coronaiion will pro- bably take the hint so appropriately and setsortably tendered by King Edward himself, in his communi- cation to tne Loudon Mayors of a desire to entertain half -a-million of their poor people t 3 a dinner on ' that rare and auspicious occasion, while they may also benefit to some extent in completing their arrangements for:the festive:portion of the rejoicings by the experiences of the then Princess of Wales ati the time of the Diamond Jubilee of Queen Victoria. I Like the " fine old English gentleman " that he is, " while he feasts all the rich," King Edward shows I that he " ne'er forgets the poor," any more than I does his Royal consort, Queen Alexandra. if, as in I London in 1897, some provincial millionaire would now come forward witn a ..V.15,000 cheque," anony- mously" tendered or otherwise, the ways and means of feeding multitudes would soon be devised, I although the remaining difficulties would be of no I illusory character. tqupendous, indeed, would be the task, even when lightened by division and sub- divi,ion of authority. In London, of course, each of the boroughs will look after its own dinners,and so confusion and congestion should be avoided. It is in centralisation that the mischief on these occa- sions lies, and it was more or less centralisation that proves to be the:drawback of the scoeme carriedout nearly five years ago. The cost of this latest gigantic undertaking, in the shape of a feast, is estimated at £30,000, showing a generous proportion per head. But it has to be remembered that a con-idern.ble part of the money will be ex- pended not on the viands alone, and on the pur- chase of the propoSecl aouvenir cups, but on the preparations for serving them to the guests. Accord- ing to the King's wish, the dinners will be served simultaneously.

Tuesday's report concerning Si Patrick's Day celebrations in the English provinces, made one think better of his country. To people now living there has always been an Irish question, and the Irish question promises to be with es for many years to come. But though some of the celebra- tions assumed a political lorm,of which no one has a right to complain, it was distinctly observable that their plane had been raised. Scotchrnen have their Burns's Night and their St Andrew's Day in every town of any repute in the English provinces, and it is customary and well for Irishmen in like manner to remember their native land. It is good to cherish the sentiment of nationality, and the predominant partner, personified in John Bull, can afford to not merely tolerate but to encouraza the eeleorations alike of St Patrick and St Andrew. For he knows that his blood is being rapidly mixed by the steady inflow of population both from Ireland and Scotland, to a degree that makes him more cosmopolitan every day.

Severe economists, under cover either of trades- unionism or of Socialism, are raising the question whether there is any need for the elaborate func- tions which, according to the accounts in the daily papers, are to mark the coming Coronation. Why these people do not press the inquiry on their own account, instead of endeavouring to make the trades-unions responsible for it, may be best known to themselves. But it is not necessary to identify ourselves with Toryism because we say the answer to the query is obvious. In the first place, every- thing is to the good that adds to the dignity of toe Crown, and brings the Sovereign into touch win the people; secondly, the King and the Queen are the leaders of Society, and it is to their Majesties that Society looks for guidance and encouragement and, thirdly, the hol ling of a Curt on a scale so elaborate as was described the other day, for irc- stance, means the circulation of vast sums of money. The really economic view of a Court in which all the ancient glories are to be revived should furnish a multitude of arguments in favour ot rehabilitation and maintenance. "The pomps and shows and high solemnities" are therefore juAitied in many ways, and more particu- larly at a juncture when Queen Alexandra has issued the recommendation, which should be a command, that preference should be extended to all articles of personal and general adornment that are produced within the confines of the Empire. Even from this standpoint such as it is, the local Trades Council, or the indivi- dual members whe raise their voices against the magnificent State functions contemplated,only pro- claim thereby their own shortsightedness and lack of anpreciation of the true principles of economy. The effect of this thought! ul recommendation on the part of her Majesty will be more fully experi- enced when the whole country,and even the Empire itself' dons its festal garb for the Coronation. The action of a trades council, or union, that would dis- courage the public celebration of such an event as awaits the nation and the Empire in June next, can only be likened to that which has led to the abandonment of the King's proposed visit to the sister cpuntry, and to a consequent loss in hard cash which is estimated at two millions sterling ! The .Sum may seem gigantic, but it is possibly within the mark. Are they the friends of Ireland who do this kind of thing ? And are they the friends of Englandor of the people in the provinces who would curtail the Coronation celebrations, make them unprofitable to the multitude at home, and a laughing-stock for other and unfriendly natiods ? The narrow-minded view of the "sophist, the economist, and the calculator," is in this instance at least, fallacious.

Butter or water? That was a question asked in the House of Commons the other day, and is one that has been before the country for some time past. The Minister of Agriculture said he should fix a butter standard as soon as those most concerned have had an opp )rtunity of seeing the evidence submitted to the Butter Com mlttee, which evidence was expected to be issued in a few days. But,

without waiting for any further report, " those most concerned" have probably come to the con- clusion that a 16 per cent. water standard has been practically agreed upon. This will be much better than as matters stand at present, when, according to a recent judgment in the High Court, it has been practicilly decided that butter containing the utmost quantity it can be made to hold may still be sold as genuine. Ye Git will leave much to be desired. Indeed thelßutter Standards Committee, judged by an interim report which it recently issued recommending the adoption of this 16 per cent. limit, might as well not have sat at all. If the recommendation is adopted the assumption must be that the poor man shall pay for at butter rates, and " eat "water. because the tradesman has a notice at his door to the effect that all butter sold at his establishment is diluted with water. Factor- ies, with all their methods and appliances for work- ing water into butter, will not be handicapped in the least, while the proprietors of cheap restaurants will be able to 5,311 watered butter, like margarine, with perfect immunity.

Members of the different Regiments of Yeomanry scattered over the country have within the past week been much exercised about the orders receiv- ed for the annual training on Salisbury Plain. The action of the War Office in calling upon so many troops to encamp at this great centre of military training is evidence, no doubt, that the authorities are determined to increase the practical irnowle.ige of the Yeomanry, and this fact is generally and cheerfully acknowledged by the men ; hut to be under canvas from May 7th until May 24th, as the troops in question are expected to be, at a spot which in most cases means a great distance from home and business, is likely to prove exceedingly inconvenient. Probably more will be heard about the arrangements very shortly, and in the mean- time efforts are being pat forth to persuade the authorities to make them as easy as the circum- stances will allow. It is very desirable, for many reasons, that the muster roll should be as large as possible this year, and if for no other consideration than this, the authorities should not require a good deal of persuasion.

THE LATE CAPTAIN SAVAGE. THE FUNERAL. ADDRESS BY THE REV. JOHN GUNNELL.

Tee grave closed over the remains of C aptaiu Savage on Friday afternoon. Pr:or to the finterment at the cemetery,a memorial service was held in Bswsey- to d Wesleyan eeapel, c onduece roe the Rev J Guneelt (supernumerary, stationed at Penketh), and the Rev C H Elloken (minister at Bewsey-read Wesleyan Chapel). The coffi I was barna on the shoulders of the bearers to the communion rails where it rerte I during the service. Two beautiful wreaths were placed at the bead of the coffin. The service commenced with the singinglof the hymn "0 God our help in ages past." The frst portion of the barisi service wis then read and another hyma "Give Me the wings of faith to rise " was sung. The Rev Jahn Guanell, in the c ,ursl ef an apdreeia- tive ader ,ss,Trid they most not forget that that was a Christian burial. Their dear friend who had been called away from than was a Christian man. Rio soul Wae nourished by Christian hopes. They, his friends, were gathered round his bier to pay him that mark of respect As Christian people they sorrowed—they could not help sorrowieg—britltheirs was not a hopeless sorrow and it FErm9d dedrable when they gathered together under such depressing circumstances, to direct their attention to the brighter side of the question. Nowhere in the New Testament was there to be found the repulsive representation of death that men bad invented. Death WAS spoken of in the New Testament as "falling asleep." and the death of a Christian man was simply falling asleep. They had to face the fan thst another of the old standard bearers had fallen ; fallen thank God to rise again, Ano'her mariner had c Ist anchor within the &mired haven. He did not know hew these things affected those who woes getting on in life but sometimes it made him (the preacher) fel lanai}... Many of his friends bad gene and life became lonely as one came nearerland nearer to the grave. OF course the blow came very heavily to those who were listening to his voice and he would like to call attention to one or two mitigating circumstances. First of all there departed friend had reached the appointed man of human life—three score years and ten. They knew what the Psalmist said ateout life tpyond that period being " labour and sorrow." And so it was. It was a sad thine to out-live one's generation and one'a use- fulness. Their friend while be had reached and even passed the appointed span had not grown into feeble- ness or anything apprea,ching second child-hood. Was not that some mitigation of the blow ? Then he was in very active service almost te the last. Nimble in step, cheery in speech and quick in hla manner he remained so until within a few days before he passed away. There was no lingering Mose Furthermore he was respected by all who knew him. His memory was held in the very highest esteem. Were net these consolatory to those immediately connected with their departed friend and did they net come with some com- forting force to every one there ? They sorrowed hut not without hope. Th .re were some people who spoke of death as bnieg a "leap in the dark." It was not that, Rather was it a journey through an underground passage to a world of life. Their friend was now living and rejoicing in that life. After prayer,the organist (Mr WaiSworda) played th Dead March in "Saul."

The procession to the Cemetery was then rc-!ermad. At the broad was a carriage containing the Rev J Gunnell, Rev C H Hocken and Mr F Pendlebury. the was folowed by an open landau displaying a choice col'ection of floral tributes. Immediately following the hearse were the carriages containing the relatives and friends. The chlef mourner present was Captain W A Savage (sco). LECTURE ON DOGS.

On behalf of the Halton-road football club a lecture was given on Monday evening by the Rev Alfred Wells on " Cave Canem " (Beware of the dog.) TnPre was a good attendance. Mr S Taylor presided and in the course of a vigorous speech re- marked that while he was in sympathy with physi- cal exercise he strongly protested against the introduction of continental customs in this country The lecturer in a most interesting address illustrated by limelight views described the lives of dogs in this country as compared with astern countries. Like human beings there were two kinds,good and bad. The eternal law governed the existence of both that one should grow like the persons whose company he kept. He urged that great dlscretion should be exercised in the choice of companions. A vote of thanks was accorded the chairman, the lecturer and the organist (Mr il Foster) at the con- clusion of the meeting. RUNCORN LIBRARY COMMITTEE MEETING.

The monthly meeting of the Library Committee was held on,Tuesday, Mr T Pope in the chair. Mrs Jones, the librarian, reported that the total issues for the month of February amounted to 2305 in the lending department sad 77 in the reference depart- ment being an increase of 208 and 31 over the corresponding period last, year. The issues repre- sented theology, 36, philosophy, 13 ; science, 84 ; history, 145; poetry, 17 ; fiction, 1812; French, 3 ; music, 14 ; miscellaneous, 241. Tee report was adopted.—lt was resolved that 150 volumes he re- bound on the motion of Messrs Handley and Riley, A vote of thanks was accorded Mr Hatton for com- piling a list of books in the library on Cheshire for Sir John Brunner.—Proposed by the chairman seconded by Mr Cunninghane.—Mr Handley an- nounced that by next week he expected they would have the publications of the Chester Archeological Society since its formation fifty years ago. tie pointed out that the volumes pos- sessed extreme interest and thought it would be well for the committee to be affiliated to some historipal society in order that they might obtain their dnnual volumes. A subscription to the Cheshire and the Lancashire and Cheshire Societies of lOs 61 yearly would enable them to obtain volutnee which would cost the outside putlic 363. Resolved to keep the matter before them for a future meeting.— On the motion of Messrs Riley and Hatton it was decided that the library should be closed on Thursday und not re-opened until Tuesday afternoon.

RUNCORN NOTES.

[By A CORRESPONDENTI The beautifying of Runcorn proceeds apace. Not content with planting paplars along different roads the local authority has now monopolised the only decent space at the public dispwal and partially transformed it into a flower garden. While cordi- ally appreciating all efforts to stimulate culture and a love of the beautiful and picturesiue among the middle classes a shade of disappointment can- not be suppressed that the Devonshire Square which has been the ground of so many religious political and convivial assemblies will now, practi- cally be bur the " shadow of its former greatness." One remembers that on such festive occasions as Whit Monday, the square, being the largest and , most central space, was the ncurally rallying ground of all the clubs and Sunday schools. Now, however, that a huge triangular piece of ground , has been enclosed by a wire fence, which may be 1 useful but decidedly not ornamental—that is from the standpoint of that art which the originators of the scheme are desirous to promate—the friendly societies and religious organizations will have to find some other spot for trooping their colours.

To a certain extent this bit of garden fresh from the country which will be with us all the year round, will compensate for this drawback. Ere long the poplar, cedar and ash will proudly uprear their heads amid screens of leafy ffliage. Thb tall nacturtium,the brilliant canary and the voluptuous virginia creepers will aid their cunning and intricate thral designs. The humble violet, the delicate pansy, the beautiful rose will throw their lustre and fragrance around. All of which will transform The prosy, dull leaden-hued square of old into*a veritable fairy paradise.M.eanwhile when even ing descends sweet music iwill be discoursed from the vicinity of the shrubs and flowers, while cats innumerable will warble their praises to the moon for such a happy hunting ground having been pro- vided them gratis. Thus Runcorn moves one step further in the process ofevolution

There is sure to be a stiff contest in some of the wards. It is almost certain that three new men Messrs AR Norman,J P., J Hdtsefiald and J Shaw, will seek local honours. The latter gentleman. it is interesting to note is the nominee of the Co- operative Society. And his return is almost certain provided all members of the Society vote solidly for him. It has been stated that the Society having felt aggrieved at the action of the Council respect. ing the question of private slaughter houses has determined to have a voice in the management of the town's affairs. And as they are about the fourth largest ratepayers it seems only fair they should have a part in the deliberations of the city'fathers. It has been stated, too, that it is the Society's intention to run a nifin pledged to their interest at least annually. If this be done— and there is no reason why it should not be dove, Feeing how strong the Society is numerically as well as financially—it is only a question of time before there will be a co-operative majority on the Council. And then the town will be pledged against the individualistic policy with a vengeance.

The Salvation Army are to he congratulated on the result of their self-denial week. The majority of the townsfolk will be astonished to learn that under the superintendency of Captain Younz the total reached the very substantial sum of over £4O being an increase of over £23 on that of last year. Salvation Arrnyites ere noted for their ability to squeeze blood out of stones but rarely has it been demonstraPed better than on this occasion. It is positively astonishing that Runcorn in its present state of poverty should contribute such a magnifi- cent sum even to so worthy an object.

FOOTBALL.

With Runcorn drawing away in the first round of the cup ties it became necessary for the match to be replayed on the trwell-lane enclosure. The mid. week match created n-i little interest and a largs body of supporters journeyed with the team which has generally managed to give the 'green 'uns " rather more than they bargaine 1 for on their own ground. All day a strong wind had been blowing ' and the supporters of the home contingent breathed a sigh of relief when the sp;n of the coin decreed that Runcorn were to play with the strong and ever freshening breeze. The opening stages of the con- test made it evident that the tactics of the visitors were to keep the leather close and waste time while the " Linnets " chose to play the fast and open game. In spite however, of the dogged deter- mination of the Leighites the attack of Houghton and his merry men was swift and keen. Between the front and rear divisions there was a capital understanding and the combination all round was simply superb. The forward work left nothing to be desired and the rear rank completely disorgan- ized their opponents. Try as they would Leigh could make no headway even in the second half and it was but once or twice that the home colours were even in danger of being lowered. It was simply science versus force and science won. For the visitors Pearson, their energetic full back, !played a fine game, and without his judicious assistance a much heavier score would have had to be recorded. Their halves and threequarters shaped but indifferently at best and never once got into their proper stride. On the other hand the Run-

r cora forwards worked like Trojans and headed by Walker were like deers after tne leather. Jolley played a wonderful game considering that early on be had to retire for some time, badly hurt, while the three quarter line combined in a manner that will make the pride of Spindle-tom look lively if they intend to pile up a score on Saturday. Hough- ton came out too, with flying colours, and, with one or two exceptions, never made a mistake. A word should also be said in praise of Flannagan who played on thawing as neat and safe a game as could be desired. It is satisfactory to note that both tries were the result of combination and sound judgment demonstrating that the passing machinery is as satisfactory as ever in spite of our Manchester critic. The goal obtained by Richardson within the last flve minutes filled up the cup,inasmuch as Leigh had been shying at the posts on every conceivable opportunity. In spite of t,wo or three regrettable occurrences in the shape of disabled men it was a very fair game and everyone must admit that the best team won.

On Saturday,Runcorn Meet Oldham in the second round at home. The struggle will be a keen and interesting one but I think our team will manage to win comfortably with the advantage of playing on their own ground. Oldham are not the team they were and Runcorn's improved form makes a hard nut to crack.

It is stated on excillent authority that a deputa- tion from Oldham waited on the Runcorn Com- mittee at the conclusion of Wednesday's match and asked that Saturday's match should be played at Oldham, offering at the same time £lOO as a golden bait. The committee wisely refused the tempta- tion and the match will take place at Runcorn as previously arranged.

The committee have selected the following team to meet Oldham : Houghton, back ; Jones, Butter- worth, Warder and Flanagan, three-qnarters; Jolley and Richardson, halves ; Walker, Langley. Woods, Moulton, Farmer, Reece, Brookes and Higgins, forwards.

NOTES BY THE NAY AT EAR LESTOWN. [By A CORRESPONDENT] FOOTBALL.

The finest match played on the Earlestown

ground this EeAson was that which took place on Satur lay between the home eleven and Darwen. The day was beautifully fine, but a very strong wind blowing obliquely across the ground greatly interfered with the play, Thrweu '.re an unbeaten team, but they were in 2rtlVe daub; as to whether they would keep up that standard after they had visited Earlestown. Consequently they brought their strongaist eleven,together with a sp-tcial train load of supporters. When the teams lined up it could be seen that they were both keenly eager and the spectators knew they were in for an exciting time. Nor were they disappointed. Earlestown had to face the hill and the wind, and from the I kick off a very fast pace was set. Unfortunately , for Damen one of their backs twisted his knee early on, and he had to retire for a considerable ' time. They however pluckily stuck to their guns and both goalkeepers bad some exciting periods. The men between the sticks were safe, and un- doubtedly saved their respective sides from defeat.

Earlestown were the first to score, and the shot was one with which Armstrong the Darwen custo- dian did not attempt to deal. In fact Danny Whitehouse reverted to an old dodge to mislead the goalkeeper, in which he was successful, and he banged the ball into the net. It was a clearly obtained goal about which there was not the element of a doubt. The equalising goal was not obtained until the second half and the curious point about it is that it was rnt a goal at all.

The referee, however, gave it in as a goal but then he evidently did not see the play.' was stand- ing behind the posts, and saw the ball shot in from the left wing. it was sailing across the goal mouth, and would have gone outside, but one of the Dar- wen players rushing up, met the ball and with up- lifted hands knocked it into the net. Of course several players were clustered about the goal and the referee could be forgiven for his mistake,which, however cost Earlestown a point. Fairly and squarely, Dar wen were beaten, which several of their supp nters acknowledged, but as one of them said " You don't know the Dar wen tricks."

Newton had a slice of good fortune on Saturday. They met Birkenhead on the latter's grouni, and succeeded in defeating them by two goals to one. Newton want a few victories to put them in a re- spectable position in the Combination table.
