Alleged Misrepresentation.

Case at the Warrington County Court.

At the Warrington County Court, on Tbursday,before bis Honour Judge B3wen R3wiands, 8.C., an action was heqrd in which Alfred Albert Barkpr,chemist, of 14, Markrlt-street, Earlestown, claimed £5O dames from Francis Arthur Brown, a cert. dentist practiiind; at Warrington, for frauiulent rnisrepreinntation on thr sale of a chemist's business which he previously con- ducted at Market-street, Eirlestown.—Mr E A White represented the plaiting. and Mr bongton appeared for the defendant.

In stating the plaintiff's case,Mr White told the Court thst by fraudulent misrepresentation, the defendant in- ouc the plaintiff to buy his business of , a chemist, which he alleged was worth £220 for stock and £125 for fittings, wht nag the defendant knew the stock and fivures were worth I very much less. On the 14th December last year, the plaintiff having beard that the defendant wished to dig- pose of his busine s went Co see him. He had had a very diffilult case, and it had Oben ; very well conducted by Mr White, but it was infirm in many particulars. It was impessible, of course, for any judge or jury to say exactly what did take place, and it seemed to him that, there being such conflict between the evidence of plaintiff and defendant, he ouelit not to fix the defendant with the constqllPllCeB of fraudulent misrepusentation. The whole advocacy of the case was inconsistent with a fraudulent or reckles3 purpose on the part of the defendant. With regard to the amount which plaintiff paid for the business, his Honour said he could not see that the price was an exorbitant one to give for the business as it stood, tie gave judgment for the defendant.

ST. PATRICK'S DAY.

Ce;ebration at Earlestown.

Tao local branch of the United Irish Le ,gue of Great Britain celebrated the anniversary of Ireland's patron Saint, by a concert and meeting. held in the Town Hall, Earleetown, on Saturday evening. The loyalty of Irish- men to their cause is never more exemplified than at these gatherings, when they tuna up in hundreds. Saturday night was no exception, and the hall was packed to its fullest extent. As on previous occasions a good musical programme was provided, the artistes including Madame Bella Reynolds (London and Provin- cial Concerts), Miss Emily Bailey (Manchester concerti), Madame E Toole, Mr John Higham (Earlestown), and Mr Chas. Nearey (Sr. Helens), with Miss Josephine Ruby as pianist. The Earlestown Orchestral Socls te was also present, under the conductorship of Mr A Ashley, and played two or three Irish selections such as "Sham -ock leaves," "Die Irish pilot," and Iristiiiational and Patrio- tic Airs. Most of tem voc.l c ontributionsi were Irish balladeebut the item which rooeed the enthusiasm of , the audience to the highest piech was " Toe boys of ' Wexford " sung try Madame Toole. The ether artistes acquitted themtelves efficiently and the concert was most successful throughout. After the concert, a meeting was held over which Mr Michael Flannaean(president of the leaeue branch) pre- sided and be was supported by Mr Pius J Boland, M.P., for Kerry' and Mr J O'D ud, M.P, for S igo, and most of the offici de connected with the branch. 'The Chairman assured them of the pleas,ire it gave him I, to have the honour of presiding °ter such a great meet- ing of his exited countrymen arid women who had assembled there for the purpose of protesting against the misrule of their native country. One of the great principles of the United Irish League was to cultivate British public opinion upon one of the greatest griev- ances existing in Ireland at the present time, the decrease in the population of that fertile Ithd, propor- tionate with the iocreaee in Great Britain. Be quoted figures in support of his areument. He would aak any sensible minded Englishman if he could honestly say that Ireland had been rightly governed when she had lost her population to such an extent, whilst Great Britain had increased hers. T.sey were told that if Ireland got Home Rule Scotland would want it too. He admitted that it would be advantageous to Scetchmen if they got Home Rule, but he argued that Ireland was in greater need of it than either Scotland or Wales. Sixty years ago Ireland could boast a popula- tion of 5,554,000 more than Scotland, whilst at the present time Scotland could boast of 15,411 more in population than Iraland. Taey might rag that the land had not the capacity to supply an those millions of people, whom, they said, were unfortunately compelled to leave it through the misgovernment of the coentrr. Reading over some statistic., he found according to Sir Robert Cain. that Ireland WRS capable %leder proper management of mainettin,ng 20 million souls ; according to Mr D Beamont, 25 millions, and Sir Robert Ball, who had travelled all through Ireland, 100 millions. Taking 20 millions as the number the country could support, could anYoue honestly say that the country was properly goveraed when she ()plc, supported a little Over four milions, three parts of whom lived in abject misery and poverty? He was Vbound to say that the position which Great Britain held to-lay was chiefly owing to the valour of the Irish people. Irishmen could govern every country in the werei but thfir own. What was the reason ? B.Tause they did not get the chance. They were not there that evening to go into the (por- tion of the war, but for the sake of argument he wQuld ask who snatched the position of this country out of the fire at Glencoe ?—Only Insiomen. He was not proud to have it to say, but at that critical m-ment had it not been for the valour of the Irishmen, who had been forced throlgh circumstances to j,in the Army and take part in the war in South &fries, victory that day would undoubtedly have been with the Boers. Be could say , to them in the words of that gallant patriot, who to-Jay lay on one of Mr Wyndham's plank beds in SI go gaol, Mr John O'Donnell '• we have had no grievance with the British workpeople, our grievance is with the Government." Be advised every son and daughter of Ireland to take home to themselves and families the history of the country so that in the future they would know how to argue peinte with people who were opposed to them, and show them the real merits of the question. They world then know the reason why they were fight- ing and for what reason thy had to fight. Ho asked them not t be trait ore to themselves and their cause. He took little notice of what WAS said by Lerd Rose- berg, who was cleaning his slate. A man with nothing on his slate had nothing to give, and therefore they had nothing to look forward to. (Applaus^.) _ .

Mr Glynn moved "That this' m—eeting' of Irish men and women of E srlestown and district takes this oppor- tunity of recording its most emphatic condemnation of the nefarious policy pursued by the Government in renewing the regime of coercion in Ireland. whereb7 a peaceable and crimeless people are subjected toad 'grant injustice and misrule by the quartering of hordes of police and other forces to blister up the cause of the rapacious Irish lords. The Late Mrs. Monks

Preaching in Wycliffe Congregational Church on Sunday morning on the text "Then said He, lo I come to do Thy will, 0 God" (Hebrews x, part of y 9) the R-v John Yonge made a brief allusion to the death of Mrs. Monks. " I think," he said, "we shall like to remember this morning one who has passed from the life of this town since Sunday last All these who knew the late Mrs. Frederick Monite are now bearing testimony to the true godliness of her quiet, unobtrusive, unselfish life. It matters not that she ' followed not with us,' at least in outward profession. Her views of truth and ours may have differed, but none who knew her spirit and manner of life can doubt that doing what she believed to be the will of God, doing her duty in doinp right and living truly, was the sovereign thing with her. She lived not to herself, and her depar- ture will create a void in a far wider circle than that of her own family in whose bereavement we all deeply sympathise." At Caire-atreet Chapel on Sunday morning, before the closing hymn, the minister made a brief reference to the loss which the congregation had suetained since they last worshipped together. They were, he said, all one in their deep sympathy with the bereaved family, and'in their affectionate remembrance of the friend whom, for a time, they seemed to lose. She had many claims upon their gratitude. Her liberal contribution to the support of good works ia con- nection with that place was the least of her gifts to them. As leng as her strength lasted she had Riven of her personal service, and that in no perfunctory way, but with an insight which was the result of sympathy and thought. After referring to specific instances of Mrs Monke'a activity in the Cairo-street institutions and in the town, the speaker said that she had Riven them, above all, a high example of beautiful living, of Christian character. To strength of will she added simplicity, quiet self-control and sweetness if temper. These made up a sr.il in which virtue could grow freely, and in which some of our most besetting faults could not take root. The patience with which she had borne the physical trouble of her late years was a fruit of the strength and loveli- ness of her character. The kindness which was so essential a part of her nature had a peculiar fine- ness of quality; it was no mere carele-a good- nature, but involved a ready and cheerful self- eacrifice,and a willingness to give care and thought in order to make her help effective in the best way She would live in the minds and hearts of all who knew her,as an examole of an understanding gedli- ness.—The hymn "For ever with the Lord "was then sung by the congregation.

BEFORE the MAGISTRATES

CHILDREN'S PITIABLE LOT.-MOTHER SENT TO GAOL FOR NELILECT. _

At the Warrington Poli 3e Court, on Saturday, before Mr T Birchen and other magistrates,Ada Love, of no settled abode, was charged with having been drunk and disorderly in Buttermarket-street on Friday evening and also with wil- fully exposing and neglecting her two children in a manner likely to cause them unnecessary suffering and injury to health.—Mr Browne prosecuted on behalf of the N.S.P.C.C., and said the society were much indebted for the assistance rendered in the case by Mr Thomas Rigby, who saw the woman on Friday evening with the two children in Butter- market-street. She was helplessly drunk and had been ejected from three public honses. Mr Rigby eventually saw the defendant Close the 'children in an h a manner as to cause him to call the attention of P C Millington to the matter. The prisoner was theta taken into custody and Inspector Hackett sent for. The inspector examined the children and found them la a terrible condition. Neither of them was wearing underclothing They were rosy looking in the face, but their bodies were •'like little skeletons." The children were verminous and there was lice in their hair and clothing. The youngest child's boots were in such a condition as to be crippling his feet. They told a pitiable tale. The girl said she did not want to go bad( to her mother. They left Meer- pool on Monday, and had tramped to Warrington. They had not had more than two meals a day and were out all Friday. The mother did • nothing but drink." When asked for food by the children, she struck the little boy in the mouth. c ossing it to bleed. The woman had been before the magistrates at West Riding and Manchester.—Thomas Rigby, foreman bricksetter, corroborated or Browne's state- ment.—lnspector Hackett said the boy could not stand when his booti were taken off. so sore were his feet. He took both children to the Workhouse.—The girl who is ton years of age stated that all the food they had on Friday a m- asted of breaktast in the morning and a halfpenny worth of chipped potatoes at tea time. She did not want to go with her mother (who was unkind to her and beat her).— Prisoner said she had not beaten the childred and they were not starving for food. She went- into the publichouses to sell Sowers. Th • children were worse off for clothes than anything else —She was sent te gaol for two months.

A BAD BLUNDER

All blunders are bad, bur, smos blunders are much worse than others. History abounds in records of colossal blunders, such as the ordse to charge at Bala- clava and the declaration of war against Prussia by France in 1870. The best man is the man who makes the fewest blunders, not the man who never blunders at all—b-cause toar man is not born yet Should such a phenomenon ever appear among US, we should like to know him ; his aeguaiatante would aline be a liberal education.

Toe most frequent and most dangerous blunders we make relate to ouvelves. " Know thyself," is an in- junction difficult to obey, each one of us believing all men .mortal but ourselves_ It thus arises that the first insidious approach of disease is so commonly disregard- ed in the hope rather than the belief that it will of itself pass away ; and so each year valuable lives are lost which rie,glic have been saved, just as on nearly every battlefield some bras e soldier bleeds to death for want of a good Samaritan at hand wit's a tourniquet. Mr Ge,r4e J Buck, of Caister-en-Sea, when taken ill about 12 years ago, did not make tee common blunder ef dis- regarding his condition, but consuite I a doctor, who informed him that his "lungs were going," and pre- seribed for bim accordinzly. From tbis attack Mr Buck recovered, but was esized a second time, and so recently as three years ago the gloomy report of , nine years before was repeated. " I sulered," said Mr I Buck on 20th November, 1901, "with a tightness of the chest and a sharp shooting pain at tr, heart. I became hoarse and had a troublesons3 cough, divti accumulation •F phlegm which constant eff )rts failed to remove. My appetite, at no time great, practically ceased, and the little I ate I was quite unable to digest, so that became practically a wreck. Nothing that was done for me benefited me in the •least, when one day my dear old mother said, George, why don't you try Mothrr Seigel's Curative Syrup ? I have often taken it, and always been the better for it.' To please her I obtained a bottle. It was the best in- vestment I ever made. Three bottles of Mother Seigel's Curative Syrup effected my complete cure. lam never tired of telling my friends of my experience,and to any- one whose sufferings at all resemble mine I would warmly cemmend Mother Seigel'e Curative Syrup as a sure cure."

The blunder in this case was not made by Mr Buck no by his mother. Mr Buck's lungs are still " going," aud so is the rest of Mr Buck's organization. He did no: have consumption, but had what is oftln mis- taken for it—cltarell of the stomach in other words,' dyspepsia. Which goeg to show how difficult it some- times is to know what ails us.

WOMEN AND PUBLIC WORK. ---- Meeting at Warrington Town Hall.

There was a good attendance at a meeting of ladies held in the Council Chamber at the Warring- ton Town Hall on Thursday afternoon, under the auspices of the Women Guardians' Association, which has for its object the interesting of women in public work, and the promotion of their election to Boards of Guardians, School Boards Rural and Urban Diatrct Councils —Thelchair was occupied by Mrs Pater Rylande,who opened the proceedings with a brief speeeh. She said sue telt very strongly teat the practical knowledge of women on many subjects which had to b discussed by B >apes of Guardians, Schoo Boards, Parish, Rota and Urban District Councils! might be most valuable in the making of arrangements which effected materially the health, cm-retort, and well- being of their poorer sisters. She knew a great many people objacted to women undertaking public matter and she would be the last one in the world to sueges anything that was not p rfectly legitimate for women to undertake. She maintained that this was a kind of work in which the cc-operation of men and women was mos; valuable. If they could do anything to make the homes of their poorer neighbours more happy and corn- , fortable and make their le; more easier, they ought to ' try their best t do so. By the Local Governmeet Act of 1894 women were invited to undertake this work by becoming Poor Law Guardians,Perish,Rural and Urban D strict Councillors. Eight years had elapsed since that measure became law and so far, only sixty-eight women in all England and Wales had come for- ward to offer their servicee. She believed it was mest likely that most women had not very much know- ledge on the subject and for this reason the Women Guardians' Association had been endeavouring to form , committees and to hold meetings in order to interest women in the work. Warrington was situated between two large Rural Districts, Runcorn on the Cheshire side comprising thirty eight parishes, and Warrington comprising about thirteen. There were several Urban Districts in the neighbourhood, such as Haydock, Nevaten•inselakerfield, and Leigh. No lady had yet set on a Council in the Warrington Rural Distaict, but the Runcorn district had the honour of possessing one lady member in the person of Miss Bessie Duttone who was a member of the Moore Parish Council and\ would have became a member of the Reral Council, had she obtained one vote more. Ihat vote would not have f sited her if there had been a local committee to work the election. (Applause.) Miss ASIITONe a member of the Withingten Urban restrict Council, then addressed the meeting. She said she had been interested in Local Government work for a great many years, in the first instance as secretary of the Women Guardians' Association, which office she had held from the commencement of the Amociation in Manchester. Since then she had taken part in a great many elections and had finally bean elected, two years ago, to one of the largest Camila Councils in England,—that of Withington; which was practically the largest residential suburb of Man- chester. The Council's district covered an area of nine amiare miles, and had a wry large population, 'and much of its work was very similar to that of a Town Council. Miss Aahton proceeded to speak of the work which women could do on an Urban Di Are, Council, and said it was an immensely useful work. She thought it would be useful to them if she referred to the com- mittees on which she found it most suitable and useful for a woman to serve. The chief was the Health Com- mittee, which had to deal with the drainage and sani- tary arrangements of houses and lodging houses, and the ventilation, &e, of dairies and cowsheds. The Committee also appointed the Sanitary leepectors Under the new Faltory Acts the Committee had power to inspect all tee dressmaker's shops, the laundries where machinery was not used, god the bakehouses and confectioners' shops where women were employed. This wes a great extension of the work of the Health Committee of the Withiugton Council and they had formed an entirely sepatam sub-committee on which she had been placed at her own desire, because she felt it more suitable that a woman should visit the various places where females were employed. The Health Committee also had to provide isolation for infectious diseases, and in connection with hmipitale a woman could do a greet deal more teen a man. A great proportion of the patients were amine people and she thought a woman would see , far more quickly than a man all the little details that made for comfort and happiness in a hospital. She did not think women fully realized that much putlic work wasierily a wider extension of domestic work with which they were all familiar. Too msoy people said " What is the work of a woman ? Why should a woman come en a District Council?" The men on the Withington Council were all extremely nice with her when she first became a member, but she knew they were saying behind her back " What does she want to do on the District Council, Why should a woman come to the District Council ? A woman's place is by the fire .vith her cat." (Laughter) At the end of the year, however, the men were the first to say they saw where her work was and were glad she came to assist them. The speaker also referred to work which women could accomplish on Committees which dealt with cemeteries and recreation grounds, free libraries, and technical instruction. Alluding to the latter she said that when she became a member of the Withington Council she asked to be put on the Technical Instruction Committee as she was interested in educetioa. She found the men, with the idea that they were supplying every possible want for girls and women, were running two dressmaking classes and three.ceokery classee.Them they considered amply sufficient. She pointed out to the committee that in these days women had to make their own livelihood and the committee should supply an edacation that would fit them for such positions as cashiers, typists, and clerks, with a knowledge of shorthand writing and many other subjects that the men had never dreamt of as girls' subjects. The men were very much surprised as they thought they were doing so well, and they were very much relieved when she came and said there was a great deal more they could do. Since then the girls had flocked to the typewriting classes in quite as large numbers as the boys. and there were now con- tinuation classes at which the girls might be better equipped to gain their own livelihood. Then there was the School Attendance Committee and she could net say too strongly that there should be no School Attendance Committee without women as members. There was an amount of detailed work that no man with a business of his own could possibly give sufficient time to. Mies Ashton showed what good work women might do by visiting cases and advisiog parents. She also referred to the usefulness of women as members of Parish Councils and in concluding asked that some of her hearers , mould come forward and take a share in public Work. It was a real stern duty that women should take their share as citizens of this c° , look after those interests which the m IviihnitlerY'beainridg willing, were less able to look after (Applause./ Mrs REDFORD, a member cf the Chorlton Board of Guardians, also spoke. She said that as far as hoards of Guardians were concerned, the gee/Mien could never be asked, "Is a woman suitable for this kind of work?" The worth and value of women to the Unions to which they had been elected had been so clearly Droved that the question had become absolutely unnecessary. In her opinion the work to be done was exactly the Fame, whether the Guardian be man or woman. The ratepayers, by a majority of votes, elected a Guardian to represent them, and consequently it deceived upon that individual to do his or her duty as far hfully and conscientiously as possible, irrespective of being man or woman. The speaker pro- ceeded to allude to the responsibility which rested upon Boards of Guardians of administering relief judiciously, and in such a way as not to increase the number of persons applying for it. ' Her experience had tameht her thatjlthe only sure way to administer relief satisfactorily was to try and learn as much as possible about each individual care and decide each upon its own Merits. Speaking of the direc- tions in which women could be of particular value, she said they could be of service as far as the House was concerned. Clothing, Sleeping einem modation, general

cleanliness, ventilation, and the engaging of officiate were Matteis that most women understood, or very soon csuld train themselves to thoroughly understaud. The care of the sick was also a most congenial work for women. The most inte:esting feature of the work, however, was the care of the children. To the children the Guardiane stood exactly in the rnAtion of parents, and how to best deal with the children was one of the problems with which truardians bad to wr fstle. On point on wbicts all agreed was that it was most undesirable that children shou'd remain in the Workhouse or even near,it. They must be fed, el ithed, educated, and started in life, and it was the bounden duty of Guardians to sea that this was done in the best possible way. In cencluding she expressed the hope that some ladies present would feel called upon to give themselves to public work. It was a work that brought its own reward and however much they gave they would receive far more in return. Some discussion took place and a vote of thank bay-. ing:been accorded to the Chairman and speakers, en the proposition of Miss Bessie Dutton, seconclvd by bare Edelsten, the meeting terminated.

Oa Tm•liday afternoon, at Stevens' Rotas, King street, Covent Garden, London, nine specimens of the now extinct Bri'.ish butterfly dispar, or large copper, were offered for gall and brought kigh prices, a female specimen fetching no less than £7. The nine specimen which formed patt of a. collection of Use late Mr Philip Crowly, r2alised £44 6s.

THE WAR.

Liverpool Scottish Volunteers Enter Orford Barracks.

Arrival on Saturday.

Smart, stalwart soldiers, clad in Highland ccstume, have been much in evidmee in Warrington since Saturday last, for on that day the active service section of the Bth V. B. (Scottish) K.L.R.. entered Orfcrd Barracks to undergo training preparatory to embaikation for South Africa. The official communication notifying the acceptance of the section only reached the headquarters' of the batta- lion at Liverpool on Friday morniog, so that there was little time in which ta prepare for the departure on Saturday. However, the preliminary arrangements were speedily and satisfactorily disposed of, and the men, responsive to instructions assembled at the head quarters, Highgate-street, at 10 a.m. on Saturday. Whilst here Majors Macfie, in the absence of Colonel Forbes-Bell, addressed to the members of the section a few words of advice, and reminded them that in War- rington as in South Africa the honour of the Liverpool Scottish lay in their hands. Shortly before eleven c'cicck the section left headquarters, headed by the pipe and .i.lrum band of the battalion, under Pipe-Major Mackenzio. They marched down to the station to the jaunty strains of the "Cock of the North" and thrir appearance—they still retained their Highland costume aroused the greatest pos. , sible amount of interest amongst the spectators. Cheers were raised and hats and handkerchiefs were wildly waved,•whilst the shrill sounds of the pipes and the heavy tell of the drums completed a send-off which was in every way impressive and intense. The section is in command of Lieutenant John Watson, son of the Rev -Dr John Watson, and on arrival at the seat of war will be attached to the Gordon Highlanders. The section arrived at Warrington at 11.55, and their arrival was witnessed by a large number of people. Toey were met by the Orford Barracks band, and marched to the barracks, where they will remain pending orders for their departure for the front.

===== 0002601_19020321_art0105.txt | included=true | included =====
Warrington's Fourth Contingent for the Front.

Swearing-in Ceremony.

The Council Chamber at the lowa Hall, Warrington, was the scene of an interesting ceremony on Monday when the focrth contingent rf Warrington Volunteers for active service in South Africa was sworn in by the Mayor (Mr J Charlton Parr). The men, 24 in numbn under the command of Lieutenant Willcocke, son of the headmaster of the Warrington Grammar Sehool,paraded at the Drill Hall in full marching order az, eleven o'clock. They then proceeded to the Town Hall where the cere- mony of swearing-in was proceeded with. The officers of the battalion present were :—Colone. Ridgway, Major Rome (adjutant), Major Buckton,and Lieutenant Ridgway. The Mayor was accompanied be the Deputy Mayor (Alderman Roberts), the Town Clerk (Mr J Lyon Whittle), Canon Willis, Councillor Bennett, and Councillor Smethurst. A number of ladies, includ- ing Mrs Parr, Miss Parr, Mrs Ridgway, and Mrs Roome. were also present. The relatives and Diet& of the men were allowed to witce is the ceremony. The men answered the questions on the attestation form, and then took tne following oath, repeating the worda after the Mayor :—" I---- do make oath that I will be faithful and bear true allegiauce to his Majesty King Edward the Seventh, his heirs and succeissors,and that I will as in duty bound honestly and faithfully, de- fend his Majesty, his heirs, and successors in 'person, crown and cfignity,against all enemies, and will observe and obey all orders of his Majesty, his heirs, and suc- cessors and of the generals and officers set over me, so help me God." They then signed the forms, and were each presented with a King's shilling. TheiMayme,et the concissiru of the ceremony,saiii : Lieutenant Willcocks, non-commissioned officers and men of the PI ince of Wales's Volunteers, it has been with great pleasure and pride that as Mayor of this County Borough I have sworn you in to be soldiers of the regular army because Warrington has nobly performed her part in sending so large a contribution to our fight- ing forces. You have volunteerel to go to the front to maintain the honour of your country and to protect our I rights and possessions which have been wrested from us in South Africa. You will join your comrades who have already distinguished themselves there and we con- gratulate you and appreciate your self-sacrifice and devotion. You will no doubt have hardships to endure and dangers to face but the proud consciousness of serving your country and King will brace you up for all these. Bs staunch, continued his wcrship, and stand truo to your principles. I need not , enjoin courage for I believe courage t 3 be the birthright of every Englishman, but I would urge you to let that courage go hand in hand with discipline, good training, and coolness, for these combined make the Britian soldier the best in the world. lam not sure you will be going out immediately, but whenever you go rest assured that our thoughts and our hearts will go with you. We fervently wish you farewell. God speed you and give you a safe and triumphant return. (Hear, hear). Colonel RIDGWAY said: On behalf of my battalion I thank you Mr Mayor for the very kind words you have spoken to these men. I feel sure they will do what you have enjoined them to do. Those who have gone out before them from Warrington have done their duty and have been well spoken of. lam sure they have come forward to assist in finishing thi& war as soon as pos- sible. I trust they will soon be amongst us once more, but in any case if they are not, you will have no reason to be ashamed of the meet who are going out from Warrington. (Applause.) The MAYOR shook hands with the men as they filed out of the Council Chamber. Then they assembled in front of the Town Hall steps where they were joined by the Mayor and a photograph was taken by Mr T Birtlee. At the conclusion, the men gave three cheers for the Mayor. A large crowd had assembled, and when the men set out upon their match to Orford Barracks with the Volunteer band at their bead, they were heartily cheered. The contingent will remain in barracks unti their departure for the front.

ST HELENS.

HEATH oF MR G GARDNER, OF Sr. HELENS, Mr George Gardner, of Park House. St. Ann's, a well- known fi.zure in the town, died on Wednesday after a few days' illness. Mr Gardner fcr many years carried on business as a glassbleuder and ornamenter in Hall-street, and bad reacbed the advanced age of eighty-three years. He was most highly respected, and much sympathy has been eapresied for his widow and family of eight children. The funeral will take plAce tc-morrow at 12.30, at Christ Church, Eccleston. Mr Gardner was a keen sportsman, and had seen sixty firand Nationals decided. He saw every Grand National but ono since the race was started.

CONSERVATIVE MEETING AT EARLESTOWN.

Lord Newton and Conscription,

A meeting under the auspices of the Newton Division Conservative Association was held in the Town Hall, Earlestown on Monday evening. There was a large attendance. Lord Newton presided and be was sup- ported on the platform by Colonel Richard Pilkington, M.P, T J Gillespie, W Holland, C.B F Horton, R Stone, J.P, and a large number of the rank and file of the Con- servative party in the Division. Lord Newton delivered a lengthy speech in which he reviewed the war, and dealt with the almost threadbare question of conscription, which he considered was the only method by which we ohould be able to avoid the unpreparedness in which we were found at the outbreak of the war. He thought it was a burning shame that two little reputlics should be able to hold us in check for a period of nearly three years, and his remedy for this state of things was some method of conscription. Mr C B F Borron proposed a resolution expressing confidence in the Government, which Me Catterall seconded, and Colsnel Pilkingten M.P. supported. The hon. member's speech dealt in detail with the charges brought against him a fortnight ago b 3 Mr Sam Woods in that hall. He gave most attention to the Miner's Eight flout% Bill which had been defeated by one vote. He said it deserved to be thrown out, as in the form in which it was drawn it was not by any means a fair measure' as between employers and employed. He advoca.ed leaving the miners alone and letting them work as they were doing. The resolution was unani- mously carried, as also was a resolution of confidence In Colonel Pilkington.

The Rev A A Markham, 8.A., has resigned his appointment of Acting-Chaplain to the Ist T.B. South Lancashire Regiment.

GOLDEN SQU ARE, W ARR I N GTON.

RETURNED VOLUNTEERS Supper and Smoking Concert at warrington. --- Interesting Speeches. The bandroom at the Drill Hall, Warrington, pre- sented a scene of festivity on Saturday evening when the members of the first Special Service Com- pany furnished by the 1-t V.B.S. Lancashire Re- giment (P. W.V.) assembled for their first annual supper and smoking concert. It was during the voyage home from the Cape that the idea of an annual re-union suggested itself and met with hearty acceptance, and judging from the pro- nounced success which attended Saturday even- ing's gathering, the company will be well advised in making the supper and concert an annual event Considerable artistic taste had been displayed in the decoration of the band room for the occasion, the names of decisive engagements and places associated with the present campaign figuring'prominently in the form of embellishments of the walls. There was a large attendance of mem- bers of the company and friends, and the proceed- ings passed off most enjoyably. Captain Appleton presided. and amongst those present were—Mr Robert Pierpoint,M.P.,Colonel Ridgway (command- ing Ist V.B. South Lancashire Regiment), Colonel W W Pilkington, V.D. (St Helens), Major Edelston, Major Buckton, Captain J D Fairclough, Lieuts. Roberts', Ridgway,Williamson, Willcocks and Potter (Quartermaster), Sergeant-Major Rawlinson, and Colour-Sergeant Moore, who went out with the Ser- vice Company. At a later stage Captain Hutchin- son V.D., Alderman Roberts, and Captain Smith attended the gathering.A hot-pot supper was served by Mrs Rawlinson,and ample justice was done to the repast. After supper several toasts were honoured. Captain APPLETON proposed "The King" and the toast was enthusiastically received. COLONEL PILKINGTON AND THE " FIGHTING FORTIETH." The next toast was that of "Colonel Hall, D. 5.0., the Fighting Fortieth, and the rest of our comrades in South Africa" which was proposed by Celonel Pilkington. In the course of his remarks the speake, said that Colonel Hall commanded the company for a considerable time, and he sent them a letter of praise when they returned home. That in itself ought to be a sufficient recommendation to all present as well as to those who did not go to South Africa. He was sorry Colonel Hall's health failed him later on and that he was obliged to i return. Like so many brave men he suffered from ' sickness, which was so prevalent in South Africa Now, with regard to the Fighting Fortieth, Their record had been a long and a very splendid one, The late Colonel O'Leary—(applause)—was one of the greatest favourities the 40th had had for many years, and a very gallent officer he was He believed that while the Volunteer Company were in South Africa they did their work exceedingly well. Fortunately, or unfortunately, they did not see as mum tire as they would have liked to see, but their Captain was pleased to kncw that on several occasions they were under fire, one or two engagements being under dangerous circumstances, and ha was not sorry to say that they came out of them without losing a man. (Applause,) Several, however, did succumb to disease—three or four in Warrington and one in St. Helens—and it was a source of regret that their late comrades were not able to be with them on that occasion. (Hear, hear.) They had a great deal of grumbling now about the great expense of this long and arduous war. It was certainly not pleasant to have to pay such heavy taxes and to have trade more or less injured by the long con- tinuance of the war. but those of them who had not been out to South Africa should remember that what ever they had suffered in England was nothing compared with what the army itself had to go through. (Applause). Thousands of noble men bad been killed or had fallen victims to disease. In conclusion, the gallant Colonel said that their come rades in South Africa were under the commando Captain Fairclough, and he hoped that when the war was over they would return as safely as the first and second contingents had done. (Applause.) The toast was received with musical honours. lathe absence of Major Roome through indisposition the toast was responded to by Major Edlesten who said they did not see the 40th mentioned frequently in the newspapers but to a great r xteut it was due to the fact that they did not go in for any disasters. (Hear, hear, and laughter.) At the far end of the room they saw a name which appealed to them a great deal and that was Platers. He had been recently reading a book which had been written by a French officer, who said he thought the fi.:hting at Pieters was about the best the British forces had done in South Africa, and that opinion came from an unfavourable critic. (Applause.) Continuing the speaker said the Votunteer battalion wanted to be a sort of second 40th. They had in a certain sense intermarried with the 40tbe and they had an excellent stock to build up a battalion which they hoped would, as far ;as a Volunteer battalion could resemble a line battalion, walk in the footsteps of the 40th. (Applause). There was no doubt that example was better than precept, and the men present could do more to bring the;hattalion up to a proper standard than anyone. Be shot.ld like to use that opportunity to ask them to exert every effort to;enable them to stay as long as they could in the battalion, and help their comrades to follow the example of the 40th, in whose honour he had the right for the moment to speak. (Applause.) THE SERVICE COMPANY. SPEECH BY CAPTAIN APPLETON. Colonel Ridgway,V D,then submitted the toast of "The Service Company." He said the last three years had been to him as a sort of dreani,and if in June, 1898,5ny- one had:said that a company of Volunteers would be per mitted to go out to South Africa to fight for their Queen it would very likely have been said, " That is all fiction or bosh," and yet, in 1901, the men came back, having done their duty, and they were present that evening looking healthy and strong, and ready to fight if need be for the new King. (App'ause.) Their owe , battalion had sent out no fewer than three 6 sections, and another was to be sworn in on I Monday. (Applause). The Colonel then alluded , to the anxiety of his officers to serve in South , Africa, and to the difficult matter it had been to make the selection, and in this connection Colonel Ridgway observed that Captain Appleton had only an hour, or , tali an hour rather, to make up his mind owing to the fact that the Fenior officer who had claimed the privilege of going, found at the last minute that he could not go. But Captain Appleton went, and they could not have chosen a better officer. (Applause.) Although he Cthe speaker) could not send the re.ber officers out who wished to go, some of them took the bit between their teeth and went. Three were now out—one in his third year, one in les first, anf two were going out, one for the second time, namely, Captain George Crosfield. (Applause.) These facts showed that there were men who bad not yet had sufficient of South Africa but wished to go out again. (Renewed applause) Re had received a pleasant letter from Colonel Stopford, their commander in camp last year. He had been military secrwary to General Buller—(applause)—in South Africa, and remembered well the 40th. The Colonel wrote :—" 11. D.A.Ges Hut, North Camp, Aldershot, March 11, 1902. Dear Colonel Ridgway,—Your most kind letter and the book ;which you and the officers nf the Ist V. B.P.W.V. South Lancashire 'Regiment have given me arrived here tc-day. Will you please tell the officers from me how much touched I am by their kindness, and convey to them my sincerest thanks for tbis token of remembrance of the time we soldiered together last year. It is a great pleasure to me to possess such an interesting record of the geed work the Velunteer Ser- vice Company of the regiment did in South Africa, as not only does it form a most fitting memento of the time I was associated with the battalion which furnished the company, but because it also reminds me of the time wben I hal the good fortune to take part with the company in that a most interesting march when Sir Redvers Buller drove the Boers out of Natal. I only re- gret that my association with the battalion was ea tem- porary. I can truly say that my fortnight last year at Salisbury ?lain was almost interesting and enjoyable experience. That it was co was due to the fact that the efforts to make the meat use of the time at our disposal were so ably seconded; by the non-commissioned officers and men of the brigade, and by none more so than by the battalion under for your command. I also wish to thank you personally for the very kind terms of your letter which I prize so much, that it will be preserved by me as carefully as the books which it accompanied.— Believe me, yours very sincerely, FRED. W STOPFORD," (Appla us Captain A PPLETON responded.eelt wag, he said, his very earnest desire that this annual gatheriogawhich was proposed on Board the Custodian on the way home should be a success, and his thanks were due to Colonel Ridgway, Colonel Pilkington and others who had come forward that evening ag }launch their first gathering. He thought that the gathering was a succeis. Be was sure they would share with him the regret he felt at not having with them their senior subaltern,Lieutenant H H Lewis. THE EXAMINER. FRIDAY MARCH 21. 1902

HONOURING THE DEAD

Mr R PIERPOINT, M.P., submitted "Our Wien com- rades." He said that it seemed hard to have to propose such a melancholy toast—if toast it be—at that festive gathering ; but, at the same time, he thought it would be a mistake unless that night they markedly remember- ed these who had been left behind in South Africa. As they knew, they could not engage in a war without expecting to lose men ; they could not possibly go ttrough such a long and persistent war as this bad been without finding death putting his chilly band on a great many, and those connected with bat company had lost their comrades like the rest of the Army. They always hotyd that when a great danger had to be incurred by those with whom they were intimately connected,and in whom they took especial interest,that somehow or other they would get off scot-free and conic home safe and sound. But, of course, it was sometimes ordered other- wise, and when they rightly, even on festive evenings like that, threw their memories back to their comrades who were dead,they could not forget such a aims as Cl. O'Leary,the pride of his regiment, and the way in which he died. (Applause.) Nor did they forget—and he hoped they wouldlkeep their memories green—such men as Captain Birch, Lieut. Raphael, Captain Lynch, and Lieut. Kane, and the men of the 40th. The men they had to particularly mourn in that town were Privates Blackwood, Sudlow, Fishwick, Woods, Richardson, Hodges and Grady. He knew that four men were left behind and that two had died since, and that one of Captain Fairclough's men had succumbed too. He remembered very well—and he was deeply touched by it, ha thought it was at the beginning of last year—that the members of the Company were good enough to send him a postcard wishing him " a happy new year, and thevcomplinaents of the season." He was particularly touched because it was not a common card—something that could be bought—but had been done by hand, and on it was the roll of the company, with the captain at the head, and perhaps the most touching of all was that there were two or three names entered as men who had fallen out. He would fain believe that these men who had lest their lives, bravely fighting the enemy, or succurn:nng to equally deadly disease, somehow hail a special mission given to them b maintain and preserve the honour of old England—

To preserve it as pure as itwas given They did whilst they'd breath, Nay they grasped it in death, And returned it untainted to heaven. (A pplauae.) He would ask that in honour of the dead, the toast should be drunk in silence and that" The Last Post" should be Founded.

The company drank the toast in silence and the, sounding of the " Last Post " had a very solemn effect.

" THE GUESTS."

ALDERMAN ROBERTS AND THE RELEASE OF METHUEN. Sergeant HOOXE gave" Our Guests" in place of Ser- geant Marsh, who had to leave _

- Major BIICKTON replied on behalf of the military, and said the town was proud of the Active Service Company, and he could assure them that every member of the battalion envied them'and would have been glad of the chance of going out. It seemed to him that if all the members, from the youngest, would remain in the bsttalion till the long service medal had been received they might have an opportunity which no other men could have of influencing the rest of the battalion for good. (Applause.) The Service Company had had experience others had not had, and it was that experience which would benefit the batta- lion. (Applause.) Alderman ROBERTS, also responding, said he thanked the Chairman very sincerely for the honour he had done I him in asking him to be present at that the first of what be hoped would be many h-ppy reunions of this sort. This old town to which they were all so much attached had nothing to look back upon regretfully with I regard to its conduct. He was sorry to say that in some towns the funds provided in the early excitement of the war for the maintenance of the wives, children and de- pendents, were not being maintained with that regu- larity and generosity with which they ought to be main- tained. But that could not be said of Warrington, because whenever an appeal bad been made, no matter what the object had been, so long as it was associated with the necessities ef the campaign, the people of the town had responded in a manner which did them infinite credit As long as the war lasted— and God grant it might soon be brought to an end—and as long as there was any necessity to provide funds, he was sure they would be forthcoming. He regretted that their excellent Mayor was not with them, but he was not as strong as some of them, and he had to avoid unnecessary night work. He had, however, a feeling as cordial as any of them possessed in regard to the welfare of that battalion. (Applause.) It was impossible to prophesy; the good old Yankee phrase tad it that it was not well to prophesy until they knew—(laughter)—but it was very probable the war would not last long, and one of the features which had characterized the operations of the last week to some extent went to prove that. Among the many lessons the war had taught them—and there had been some splendid ones that in their pride and prosperity they were s• ntowhat inclined to forget— was to respect the genuine Boer. He was saying noth- ing about the off-scouring of Europe, who bad dis- graced civilization by the outrages they had perpetrated, but when they remembered that a man like General Delarey bad acted as he had done, and bad liberated Methuen without any conditions so far as they knew, it gave them a better and a higher opinion of the men whom they had to fight in South Africa than they other- wise perhaps would have had. (Applause.) In con- elusion, the Alderman said it was time the war was over and that their enemies recognized the inevitable—that it was useless to hope to restore themselves to the position they formerly held. If they would recognize that fact South Africa would open up such a future es in their wildest dreams they had been incapable of imagining. (Applause.) THE FOURTH SERVICE COMPANY.

Colonel BIDOWAI submitted the health of Lieutmant Willcocks, who is to be in charge or the 4th Section of Warrington Volunteers for South Africa. He said that If the company found it necessary to do any fighting be hoped they would do their duty by the 40th and not disgrace the Ist Volunteer Battalion South Lancashire Regiment. Lieutenant Willcoctis was cne of the officers who wiehei tu go to the front when the first company was formed, and be (the speaker) was very sorry to have to deny him then. Be bad got his chance now. and he (Colonel Ridgway) felt sure that if Lieutenant Willcocks was well secondonby his men he would do his duty. Be came of a good old stock and would not disgrace it, and when he went out to South Africa he would have the good wishes of every man in the battalion. (Applause.) In r,sponding, Lieutenant Willcocks said he would do his utmost to maintain the reputation already made by the first service company and he hoped that utmost might be worthy of the traditions of the battalion. There was still plenty of room in the secton for any men wbo wished to serve, and one of his regrets was that more men who had previously been out, had not VOl- uoteered again, more of them would join the sec. tion it would be reat help. During the evening an excalent programme of songs and recitations was submitted. Mr Levi Richardson gave a pleasing rendition ef the service company song which describes some of the experiences of the men en routs to and at the front. The chorus, whieh was joined in by the company, and went with a capital swing, was as follows : Shoulder to shoulder, hand in hand, Comrades for ever and aye;. GREAT SANKEY PARISH COUNCIL

The Pollution of Sankey Brook. Winwick Asylum Sewage to be Turned

into the Brook.

A meeting of the Parish Council of Great Sankey was held in the Board Schools, Great Sankey, on Thursday evening. Mr G Thompson presided and there were also present :—Messrs T Balton, J Fulton Smith, Joseph Kilshaw, and William Taylor, with J Smith (clerk). THE *INP7ICIC SEWAGE DISPOSAL DIFFICULTY.

Tbe Chairman thought it wa.4 a great pity that tht Winwick As lum sewage difficuly• had teen discussed in cominit,me hy the Rural Dietric: Council because it 11 handle poett the Parish Council to a great extent. If they had known sooner they might have taken action. Mr II .itm did not think they could have done any- thing sooner. lie made the matter public as soon as he , possibly could. He told the Rural Council that he was sorry to see the busitv si conducted in camera. He thought it detrimental to the public interest for public matters to be discussed in private. He regretted that matters bad turned out as they had done. The Rural District Council had given their consent to the construc- tion of the sewage pipe from Winwick Asylum to Sankey Brook at Longford and the result would be that the solid matter from the Winwick Asylum would be turned into the brook, untreated- He believed the Local Government Board would not allow it. As soon as the Asylums Board 6tAit.cl the works, the Local Govern- ment Board would put their foot down and suppress the whole thing, The Rural District Council would then be called upon t ) pipe the whole length of the brook from Longford to the proposed sewage farm of the Corporation near the river side, known as Grim3haw's farm, or pipe it to the river. If the Rural Council had followed his (Mr Halton's) advice they would have entered into an agree- ment with the County Council and the Warrington Corporation for the three bodies to pay equally towards the cost of piping the brook. The cost would now fall upon the Rural District Council and it would mean an increased rate of 3d or 41 in the £ on all the townships in the Warrington Rural district. lithe solid matter were allowed to enter the brook it would be a very serious matter.

The Chairman : How will it be when the tide backs up and floods the fielns ? _

Mr Halton : It will be all over the fields. Mr Taylor : Toe firlds will be a sewage farrn then. _ . _

Mr Fulton Smithi: Has any authoritipower to dump down their sewage just where they like. Mr Halton : They cannot do it without the consent of the Rural Council and the Council have given that concession :o the Winwick Asylums Board. The Council was sdyised by Mr Jeans that they had a good case against the Asylums Board to compel them to treat their own sewage before putting it Into the brook. He could not understand why the Council had granted the concession.

The Cnairrnan thought the Lmal Government Board would protect them to a groat extent. Mr Fulton Smith : Surely we have a locus staudi to send a protest to the Local Government Lard. The Chairman thougat it would be better to leave the matter to the parish meeting and get an opinion upon it. This course Was agreed to.

THE PARISH MEETING. A Resolution of Protest Passed.

Thompqnn presided at the annual :parish meeting which was subsequently held. There was a good attendance.

The Chairman, at the outset, gave a resume of the work of the Parish Council during the past year. In commenting upon the collection and delivery of letters, he pointed out that a great improvement had been made in the ptstal facilities of the di ctrict through the action of the Council. With regard to the pail system, this matter had been discussed, but it was a question for the Rural Council and not for the Parish CJuncil. Arrangements had beer] made with the police authorities whereby the police sergeant Woald reside in Rood-lane. An attempt was made te get better booking facilities at the railway station, but it did not succeed. The Council opposed the scheme of the War- rington Corporation to erect a sanatorium at Great Sankey, and although they were not successful it. their opposition, they were assured by the Local Government Board Inspector that Lathing would be put into the brook that would be detrimental to the interests of the township. Reference was also made to the steps taken with reference to the numbering of streets, the improve- ment of the lighting of the roads, the water supply and the burial ground.

Mr Snoddy was of opinion that an economical arrangement could be made with the Penketh night- soil contractor for the removal of 'the night-soil at Great Sankey. Mr Halton stated that arrangements were being made which would be s itisfactory. At the request of the Chairman, Mr Hilton then informed the meeting as to the action of the Warrington Rural District Council in giving permission to the Winvvick Asylems Board to enter the sewage of the new asylum into Sankey Brook. Such action, be said,would be detrimental to the district and all the districts situated near the brook. It was nothing less than a scandal that solid matter should be allowed to enter the Brook from such an important institution a the Winwick Asylum. He hid opposed it fr)rn tn first but he had stood alone in his opposition at the Rural Council meetings. He was assured that the War- rington Corporation was prepared to treat the sewag which they shot into the brook, and the St Helens Ger- potation were taking steps to deal with their sewage by putting down plant. The Rural District Council were themselves guilty of putting sewage into the Brook and matters would be worse now that the Win. wick Asylums Board had been given permission to d ikewise.

Mr Tongue asked whethrr the rewage from Winwick AlyMen was being run into the brook at the present time?

MI Halton : No; It runs on the open fields near the Asylum. Mr Bnoddy suggested that the mectmg should send a strong protest to.t the Local Government Board.

_ Mr Joynson asked if there was any likelihood of other authorities joining in the protest ? Mr Halton said he believed the Corporation of War- rington would take action. Mr William Furness poitted out that it would be tbsurd to ask the Corporation to take the matter in hand before they had dealt with their own sewage which entered the brook. He suggested that the Parish Council nhould send a protest. The Cha•rrnan . Would it not be better for a deputa- tion to wait upon the Health Committee of the Warrington Corprration P . .

Mr Furness : The Health Committee ha e done all it can to stop the St Helens Corporation from putting offensive matter into the brook, but the Warrington Corporation's hands are somewhat tied owing to the fact that they put a large amount of sewage into the bro4k.

Mr Halton : I have the assurance that they intend to treat their own sewage. Mr Furness : Until they have done so it is 113 use seeing them. Mr Snoddy said the Local Government Board was bound to take note of any grievance which was submitted to them. He would move that the following resolution he passed : " That this meeting protests in the strongest possible manner against ths Rural District Council allowing the Warrington and Sankey Brook, which runs through a populous neighbourhood becoming an open sewer, and calls upon the Local Government Board to protect them from the danger of a public epidemic and hopes the call will not be in vain." Mi Unsworth seconded the resolution, which was carried unanimously. Mr Snoddy expressed his approval of the action of Mr Halton, and thanked him lot his valuable services. (Hear, hear.) The Chairman sal the Parish Council was materially assisted in its opposition against the proposal of the Warrington Corporation to establish a sanatorium by several ratepayers who were not members of the Council, but who were appointed on the Committee by the parish meeting. In moving that the committee be disbanded he would like to propose a vote of thanks to the gentlemen who asaisted the Parish Council. Mr J Fulton Smith seconded, and the proposition was carried. Mr William Farness replied. The Chairman alluded to the meeting which had been held at Warrington that day with reference to the ap- pointment of lady guardians. One of the difficulties in rural parishes was that the two offices of guardian, and rural district councillor were combined in one person. It was putting a great task upon a gentleman to take both appoint- ments aud another objection was that they hardly over found a gentleman thuroughly interested in both objects For himself he would prefer tc be a poor law guardian rather than a rural district councillor. He thought the , two offices ought to be separated and he would move a resolution asking the Local Government Board to make the two offices distinct. Mr T Halton seconded and the proposition was unanimously agreed to.

OUR LOCAL CHURCHES.

Confirmation at the Parish Church.

The Lord Bishop of Liverpool administered the rite of confirmation to 203 candidates at the Parish Church, Warrington on Saturday afternoon. There was a crowded congregation. The number of candidates pre- sented was made up as follows :—St Clement's Mission, 70 ; St George's Mission, 60; Grammar School, six ; Clergy Daughters, six ; rest of parish, 61. Bsfore and after the ceremony of laying oa of hands the Bishop delivEred helpful addresses. Theo Rector(the Rev Canon Willis) also took part in the service, and the other clergy present were:—The Revs E J Wilicocks(Grammar School), A S Cameron, G W Dickinson, and A F White.

Ebenezer Church, Buttermarket-street.

On Sunday the opening anniversary sermons were preached morning and evening by Mr T R Whiteley of Manchester. Mr Whiteley is well kown as an evangelist and soloist, and during the services kave In hie usual earnest and robust style several well-gel ctecl solos from Moody and Sankey's book, the choir and congregations heartily joining in the choruses. In the afternoon a splendid cantata entitled " Lion of Judah " was render- ed by an efficient choir, assisted by a string baud. Col- lections were made at the close of each service in aid of the church funds. On Monday evening an excellent tea was provided by the members, after which there was a grand concert consisting of specially selected music, recitations and dialogues got up by Miss Minnie Thornton, and Mr Sheath- As usual the concert was a great suc- cess. The following is a copy of the programme : Recitation, Miss tltacey," Nelly the match-girl" ; song, number of young girls, " Marionettes " ; recitation, Miss Hands, "Willie's letter " ; 'cello solo. Mr Jackson, Adagio E Tarantella ; solo, Mr T Hughes., " When other lips" ; recitation, Mr Sheath, " The sacriligeous gamester " ; duet, Miss C Dealey and Miss L Morris, " Love of life " ; solo violin, Mr A Bennett ; recitation, Miss Lucy Sheath, "Oh, Daddy, you've killed me " : recitation, Miss Rawls, " Aunt Tabitha " ; glee, Mr R Cook, Mr J H Thornton and Mr T Hughes, " Jenks' vegetable compound" ; duet, Mr A Bennett and Mr J Jackson, violin and 'cello ; solo, Mr R Cook,"Will those love trie P " ; dialogue, Miss Littlewood, Mr Hughes, Mr Wakefield, Mr R Cook, and others, "The two pictures" (Ist A drunkard's home, 2nd A telotaier's home). Mr II Thornton was chairman. Mr J H Thoretan presided at the piano. At 10 o'clock the meeting was closed by votes of thanks and singing the doxology.

Wesleyan Methodism at warrington.

Quarterly Meeting.

The quarterly meeting in connection with the War- rington VVeeleyan circuit was held in the Lecture Hall, Bewsq-road Church,on Monday. The Society Stewards of Bewsey-road (Messrs Povey and Winstanlev) provided tea. The Rev T Bishop took the chair at 6.30. There was a large gathering of members, and the Senior Cir- cuit Steward, Mr J W Hayward presented the accounts, which showed a loss of about £4 on the quarter. A long discussion took place with reference to the condition of Hatton 'Jhapel. It was eventually decided to sell the property and apply the proceeds to erecting a new chapel in another place in the Circuit, unless the Hatton people were prepared to carry on the work. The Chair- man presented his numerical statement,which showed a membership of 890. The Rev G H Carnson,of Birming- ham, and the Rev R Wilkins Bees, of Manchester, were heartily and unanimously invited to take the places of the Rev T Bishop and the Rev C H Hocken respec- tively, these two gentlemen being compelled by confer- ence rule to remove to other circuits on September let, 1902. The Chairman reported that Warrington and Blackpool were the only circuits in the Liverpool dis- trict which had not redeemed their pledges to the Twen- tieth Century Fund. The meeting determinod to be dissociated from Blackpool in this matter at any rate, and amid a scene of considerable enthusiasm the mem- bers cf the meeting promised an additional 120 guineas, thus practically completing the fund. The Chairman nominated Mr GY W Littler as a candidate for the minis- try and the meeting accepted him. The Rev C H Hocken presented the foreign hnission returns. The income for the past year was £241 12s lid" being an increase of £9 113 7d on the previous year. Miss Bessie Dutton, on behalf of the Moore Church, invited the members to Moore for the June meeting. The invitation was heartily accepted.

The Late Mr. Abraham Haworth.

Reference by the Rev. John Yonge.

At Wycliffe Congregational Cnurch on Sunday morning, the Rev John Yonge preached a powerful discourse based on the t, xt, Then said He, In, I come to do Thy will, 0 God," (Hebrews Ir., part of 9. v.) Do not let us rashly conclude, he said, that obedience to the will of God does, necessarily or invariably, mean deprival and failure and poverty even in this life. Such is not the teaching of the Bible. It is not the teaching of human history. The men who have gone forth into life with tbe fear of God before their eyes have not been always failures in the world. Not a few of them have been amour; the most successful, even judged by the world's standards of success. Last week there died in the South of France a man whose name is not I unknown among us, who lived not to himself, but to God and for his fellows. Abraham Haworth, one of Manchester's most successful sons.passed away on Mon- day at the age of 72. Ile began life in humble circum- stances entering one of the leading business houses in , Manchester as a junior clerk, passing ou and up until he became the senior partner in the firm he began with as a servant. He was a Sun lay school lad at Hope ' Chapel, Salford; then a teacher, church mem- ber, busy and devoted worker and helper and supporter, filling every office in the Church, except that of minister, and continuing until the eno I of big days a loyal servant of Christ in connection with every branch of 9i5 service. Quiet, modest, unassum- ing, r stiring, never putting on airs because be had I some to possess large wealth, ever using his riches as a trust and laying them out generously for the service of Christ and of men. It(sveted throughout the world of business, above suspicion in public life, spiritual and devout in religiou—Abraham Haworth, beyond ques- tion strove all life through to do the will of God. Man- chester mourns his lose as truly as Liverpool laments the loss of William Rathbone. Many a poor, friendless lad has teen helped in the world. Many a widow's heart has he made to sing for j ay. Many a broken-down pastor has he enabled to rest and recuperate. Many a minister's son has he helped to educate. No religious society in our bady at any rate. Was missed his assistance. Large means have found large expenditure in doing good. And never has his character either in world or church received a stain. He "served his generation according to the will of God, and now he has fallen on sleep." llt is well we should think of him to-day for he has many times dealt generously with this church. But specially we rcay point to him as an illustration of how a life devotedly obedient to the will of God does not mean necessarily a life that fails in the werld. It cen- t radicts the common meertion that to be religious means defeat in the conflict of Jib.

Hunting Accident .at Stretton.

On Wednesday, during a thirty-five minutes' tun of the North Cheshire Hunt, as Stratton, Mr Thomas Wilkinson, the stud groom to Mr Nelson, a well- known Cheshire hunting gentleman, of Hartford, met with a serious accident. His horse fell at a ditch, threw the rider heavily,and then rolled upon him, breaking two or three ribs. Dr Burton, or Frodsham, attended him on the field, and the in- jured man was removed home in a trap. The early proceedings were disappointing, as the home cover and seven acres field failed to yield. From Whit- ley Gorse, however, a fox was got away, and the hounds pushed him through Grimsditch and over the Warrington road right for Stratton. Then he I bore right-handed, and finally beat the pack close to Cobbler's Gorse, after a rather slow hunt of thirty-five minutes:

NOTES ON UAMES AND ATHLETICS.

RUGBY FOOTBALL.

By " Ovalist."

Warrington have survived the first round of the Northern Union Cup Competition, and on that fact they may congratulate themselves. Barrow were not the most formidable of opponents, on paper, but they gave a lot of trouble before they were numbered among the ranks of the slain. The form and determination they showed were excellent, and had Warrington's been anything like so goo i they would have won by a more substantial margin. I was not, however, very favour. ably impressed with the display given by Warrington, and unless they improve upon it very materially hey will retire from the Competition somewhere about sp.m. to-day. As a matter of fact it was only just the difference in class between the Northern League and the Lancashire Competition which pulled Warrington through On Saturday. Taking the standing of the two teams as a basis of comparisbn Barrow undoubtedly played the better game, and their great efforts were worthy of more substantial reward than they got.

A strong breeze marred what would otherwise have been a perfect day for football. The turf was in splen- did order, and with the sun shining brightly, the con- ditions for both players and spectators alike were highly satisfactory. The crowd was a large one and in- cluded a few score enthusiasts from the Furness dis- trict, and all round the enclosure there was a fairly solid ring of faces. Both teams turned out as adver- tised, and Mr Ashton—who refereed very capably by the way—started the game prompt to time.

Warrington had what fortune there was in the spin of the coin, and of course elected to play with the wind. Personally I had rather they lost the toss, for they would then be in a safer position in the secolid half granted that the defence had been good during the first moiety. Anyhow, Barrow were the first to defend and it was soon apparent that they meant business. Dickenson gave the ball to Fish frequently, but the wing man was marked by the Barrovians with catlike pertinacity. Time after time they bundled him in touch after good passing runs by the home backs, and !then the Barrow forwards made tracks for the Warrington line. There was danger for a minute or two, but long punting put Warrington on the offensive again. Then Allen accomplished what the conbination behind had failed to do. He got Fish in—the method being a long pass from a scrimmage in the visitors' quarter, and Fish's quickness did the rest. He got a nasty kick in the effort, but recovered under the atten- tions of a composite battalion of ambulance men, trainers, and players. One try was the sum total of Warrington's assets on change of ends.

There were a few rather dubious faces when Barrow restarted, and a Warrington supporter in my vicinity laid and lost a sixpenny cigar on the prospects of the Barrovians. True, they nearly scored in the first minute by a long dribble,but Warrington woke up after, this. A lot more dash was evident in the forward play and though the wind was against them Warrington had as much of the game as their opponents. The latter worked hard to score, but were frustrated. Once they had a grand chance, for Bowker took a penalty kick right in front of goal and missed. Had he goaled the struggle to tiny end would have been even tighter. Warrington's chief idea seemed to be to feed Fish, and Harris was almost neglected,which is not thebest Towards the close, however, Fish dashed over again after smart passing, and there was no question as to the result after this. Barrow strove hard but had to suffer defeat by 2 tries to nil. --- What superiority Warrington had was in the three- quarter line, where the quartette showed more speed and combination than their opponents, who were not all up to Bowker's standard. The latter played a splendid game at centre and deserves high praise. Dickenson and Fish both performed well, but a better understanding between them will conduce to greater effectiveness. Ratcliffe was not quite as good as in the previous match,but was by no means a failure, while Harris lacked opportunities only. The full backs were about on a par, and so were the halves, with Hocken- hall perhaps the best of the bunch. It was forward where Warrington cut a poor figure. In the packs they held their own but their energy outside was nothing compared to that of the Furness men. Led by the ever- green Treweek they followed up with untiring vigour and calmed Warrington a lot of anxiety. The home forwards, with the exception of Morrison and Boar 1- man were fearfully slow, and in following up a big im- provement is necessary.

A tip in tactics may be worth something to Warring ton, though they should not need it. "Let the backs feed Harris more. He is worth it, and the move also gives Fish more scope when opportunity comes his, way. Though there was a fair amount of open play, the game was not of the highest order. Scrimmaging: was far too common, and it may surprise people to know that a record was kept of the number of " serums" in the game, and the total was 84—a little over one per minute.

The best performance of the day was Swinton's narrow victory at Halifax, and the odds are that they will eventually meet Broughton Rangers in the course of the tournament. The winner of that tie should win the cup. Of course Warrington, and Bradford have a say in the matter yet, and a number of others alsd.

Warrington v. Bradford is the tit-bit of to-day's cup-ties,and a fine game may be confidently relied upon should the weather be at all favourable. The teams have already met twice this season and each won on its opponents' ground, so that there is not much evi- dence to be found here for the purpose of picking the winner of to-day's encounter. Personally I, favour Warrington, not because they are a better team—which is problematical—but because they have not yet been defeated on their own ground in a cup tie There is a , good deal in this and the home team may be relied upon to strain every nerve in order that the valued record may remain unsullied. Bradford will take a lot of beating but let the forwards and backs of Warrington play with ' the same irresistible dash as on the last occasion that the two teams met in a cup-tie and the result will be the same. Forward, in particular, lam expecting a big improvement on previous displays.

The home team is, with one exception, identically the same as last Saturday, Aldred drops out of the for- wards and Edmondson fills the vacancy. The fifteen is as follows :—Hallam, full back ; Fish, Dickenson, Ratcliffe and Harris, threequarters ; Hockenhall and Allen, half backs; Motrison, Boardman, Edmondson' Fell, Cunningham, Taylor, J. Swift and W. Swift, forwards.

On Thursday I saw the Warrington captain in town still limping from the injuries to his knee. He has left Allison s, and is now visiting Mr. Jones of Liverpool, and hopes to be sound again in a few weeks. Of course he will not play football again this season,

The referee in the match with Bradford will be Mr It H Farrar of Halifax. Touch judges Mr A Critchley ( Widnes), and It Abbey (Stockport.)

Wigan deTeated Widnes in the South-West Lan- cashire League at Wigan on Wednesday. This brings the competition to a very interesting stage,Warrington, Widnes and Wigan all being in the running. Warring- ton play Wigan at home and away yet and these matches should settle the destination of the Cup.
