Cycling. I have received a copy of the Warrington Cycling Club's handbook for the coming season by the courtesy of Mr E Pickavance, jun, and as usual it is a tasty little production which does the club credit.

The opening run will take place to-day,to Warburton, leaving headquarters at 230 p m. In the event of rain, members will proceed by the 3 35 train from Bank Quay. Those members wishing to attend the football match with Bradford first, will have a convenient train from Arpley Station at 517 p in. The programme for the season, I can readily believe, is the most attractive yet compiled for the members of the club, comprising two bowling mains, several bowling handicaps, fishing match, bowling match and inter club-run, ladies' day, president's day, and runs for week-end tours to the finest and most popular cycling resorts in Lancashire, Cheshire, Derbyshire, and North Wales. I wish the Warrington Cycling Club a most prosperous and enjoyable season.

Cycling has, of late, been very pleasurable and one oannot reasonably quarrel with March weather so far. a far. Sunday was a superb day, and in company vn friend I took the opportunity of cycling over to Bux- ton and back. This is rather a stiff opening to the season's: riding but "off and on" practice during the winter had tightened up the muscles to a pitch uffi- cient for the strain even of the famous " Cat and s Fiddle." The roads were on a par with the weather, and the run to Macclesfield, via Knutsford and Chel- ford, helped by a north-west breeze could not have been finer.

We did not start until after twelve noon so that on arrival at Macclesfield the inner man clamoured loudly foridniner. Mine host of the " Angel" catered ad- mirably—so well indeed that we were totally unfit for

the seven mile grind to • the " Cat " when we started. After a mile or so of "collar work" our pride was calmly stowed away for use on a more fitting occasion, and instead of cycling we walked the next mile, stopping to take an occasional snap shot in the glorious sunshine. Then on saddle again and up-up-up till our faces would have shamed even the ruddy beetroot, but we improved in wind and leg-power, and reached the famous hostel on the top at a rattling pace. How many way-fsrers ":pass by on the Other aide," I wonder! It was more than we could do after such a warming, and in we went for the much-needed refresher. There was a char-a-banc party from Buxton inside and they appeared far thirstier than those few who, like our- selves, had come up by their own exertions We left them there, char-a-bane included.

After a brief stroll on the moor and a look at the surrounding hill tops' we departed,for the wind at that altitude waslrather toe thin for comfortable lounging. Right from the door of the "Cat" we free-wheeled, and such a free-wheel! Ye Gods! Minutes hardly appearel to have elapsed before we were in Buxton wiping the water from our eyes. It was a glorious coast. The road perfectly clear, hard as adamant, and a slope that takes you down at a delirious rate. It was the fastest four miles I have ever done, and my companion who has won many a prize on the path, could not recollect a speedier burst. And all without pedalling, except for one little rise just before you dip down out of sight of the.inn.

Time was rather short so our stay in Buxton was necessarily brief. After a look round we faced about and by matual consent began another climb on foot. The gradient here is pretty stiff and with the breeze blowing down from the summit walking was certainly the best way of getting up. Once past the railway arch two-thirds of the way up and we felt ready for the remainder per bike and soon reached the Inn once more. The shades of night were falling pretty rapidly, and at 6-42 we commenced the long descent into Macclesfield. With reliable brakes we were inclined to let the machines go, but some of the corners took more than a little negotiating. However, we were soon down and half an hour from leaving the top were inside , the " Angel " again and ordering a tea which we felt we fully deserved.

An hour or more later we set Off on the concluding stage of the journey. The new moon was Railing high in the starry heavens, and with the breeze gone com- pletely, we hummed along at a good pace. Knutsford was reached in about 40 minutes,but from here we took matters rather more lazily, for with sixty miles behind us we were not as springy at the knees as we had been. There was no fatigue however, and we reached home just comfortably tired after an outing that will take a. lot of beating no matter how good a cycling season we are favoured with.

There will be plenty of night-riding for some time to come and it behoves the night-birds to have reliable lamps. One of the best I have seen anywhere is the new" Edlite "which Messrs Miller of Birmingham have put on the market recently. It is excellently con- structed with all parts easily accessible, and it gives a very powerful light if proper oil is used. Some riders are apt to be careless about the latter detail and then blame the lamp, which is hardly a fair proceeding. The" Edlite " will give satisfaction, I feel sure, for in addition to fulfilling its purpose so admirably it is very handsomely finished and sets the "bike "off wonder- • fully.

pRUDENTIAL ASSU 3ANCS COMPANY, LIMITED. CHIEF °Felon HOLBORN BARS, LONDON. Summary of the Report presented at the Fifty-third Annual Meeting, held on 6th Marc),, 1912. ORDINARY BRANCH.—The number of Policies issued during the year was 76,831, assuring Use sum of £7.575.935, and producing a New Annuel Premium Income of £389 776. The premiums received during the year were £3,489,955, being an increase of £167613 over the year 1900. The Claims of the year amounted to £1,563.775. The num- ber of Death. was 6,723, and 6,105 Endowment Assurances matured. The number of Policies in force at the end of the year was 651.558. INDUSTRIAL BRANCH.—The Premiums received during the year were £5.523 461, being an increase of £81,164, The Claims of the year amounted to £2,104 939. The number of Deaths was 211,670, and 2,227 Endowment Assurances matured. The number of Free Policies granted during the year to those Polley holders of five years standing who desired to discontinue their payments was 74,798. the number in force being 771,279, The number of Free Policies which became Claims during the year was 17 880. The total number of Policies in force at the end of the year wa 14,334520; their average duration exceeds nine and a half years. The Assets of the Conspanv both branches, as shown In the Balance Sheet, are £43 292,' 25, being an increase of £3,396,698 over those of 1900. The Staff Provident Fund, which was founded in 1898 for the benefit of the outdoor staff, shows a satisla3tory Increase for the year, the total amount standing to the credit of the Fund being £101,930.
